Applicant	IDORSIA PHARMACEUTICALS LTD
Dosage form(s)/formulation(s)	TABLET
Dosing regimen	12.5 mg once daily, (b) (4)
Applicant-proposed indication(s)/population(s)	Apocitentan, in combination with other antihypertensive drugs, is indicated for the treatment of hypertension, to lower blood pressure in patients who are not adequately controlled on other drugs
SNOMED CT code for proposed indication disease term(s)1	38341003 (Hypertensive disorder, systemic arterial (disorder))
Regulatory action	Approval
Approved dosage (if applicable)	12.5 mg once daily
Approved indication(s)/population(s) (if applicable)	TRYVIO, in combination with other antihypertensive drugs, is indicated for the treatment of hypertension, to lower blood pressure (BP) in patients who are not adequately controlled on other drugs
SNOMED CT code for approved indication disease term(s)1	38341003 (Hypertensive disorder, systemic arterial (disorder))

UGT	uridine diphosphate-glucuronosyltransferase
ULN	upper limit of normal
WD	withdrawal
L38: ## I. Executive Summary
L39: ## 1. Summary of Regulatory Action
L40: On December 19, 2022, Idorsia Pharmaceuticals Ltd. submitted an original NDA for aprocitentan for the following indication: “Aprocitentan, in combination with other antihypertensive drugs, is indicated for the treatment of hypertension, to lower blood pressure in patients who are not adequately controlled on other drugs.”
L41: Aprocitentan is an endothelin receptor antagonist (ERA) that inhibits the binding of endothelin (ET)-1 to ET_A and ET_B receptors. ET-1, via its receptors (ET_A and ET_B), mediates a variety of deleterious effects such as vasoconstriction, fibrosis, cell proliferation, and inflammation. ET-1 has been implicated in the pathogenesis of hypertension, including mediation of endothelial dysfunction, vascular hypertrophy and remodeling, sympathetic activation, and increased aldosterone synthesis. Of note, aprocitentan has the same chemical structure as the active metabolite of macitentan, which is approved for the treatment of pulmonary arterial hypertension.
L42: In support of the proposed indication, the Applicant submitted the results of an adequate and well-controlled multipart, Phase 3 study (PRECISION) in adults with a systolic blood pressure \ge 140 mmHg who were prescribed at least three antihypertensive medications. The study included a 4-week double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period in which 730 subjects were randomized 1:1:1 to aprocitentan 12.5 mg, 25 mg, or placebo once daily (Part 1), a 32-week period in which all subjects were treated with aprocitentan 25 mg (Part 2), and another double-blind period in which subjects were rerandomized to receive either aprocitentan 25 mg or placebo (randomized withdrawal/Part 3).
L43: The review team has concluded that the application provides substantial evidence of aprocitentan's effectiveness in reducing blood pressure when used in combination with other antihypertensive drugs in subjects who are not adequately controlled on other drugs. Numerous antihypertensive drugs, from a variety of pharmacologic classes and with different mechanisms of action, have been shown in randomized controlled trials to reduce cardiovascular morbidity and mortality. Hence, the Agency accepts blood pressure lowering as a validated surrogate endpoint and basis for traditional approval of antihypertensive agents. The product's efficacy in lowering blood pressure was demonstrated in both the initial double-blind treatment period as well as in the randomized withdrawal period of the Phase 3 study. As such, the study is considered to be the scientific equivalent of two clinical investigations.
L44: As previously noted, aprocitentan has the same chemical structure as the active metabolite of macitentan and the results of safety analyses were as a whole consistent with risks that might be expected given the known risks of macitentan and other endothelin receptor antagonists. Because of the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity, aprocitentan will only be available through a restricted distribution program under a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS).
L45: The Applicant has proposed use as an add-on therapy in patients with uncontrolled hypertension and given the product's safety profile, aprocitentan will not be approved as initial (first-line)
L46: therapy for the treatment of hypertension in adults. Although it is acknowledged that other members of the endothelin receptor antagonist class are indicated as first-line therapy for other diseases (e.g., IgA nephropathy, pulmonary arterial hypertension), the benefit risk considerations are different for essential hypertension for which there are multiple other therapeutic options with better safety profiles.
L47: (b) (4) the Applicant evaluated two doses in their Phase 3 study, (b) (4) the 12.5 mg dose will be approved (b) (4). A shallow exposure-response profile was observed in the overall population. However, variability was observed suggesting that if the Applicant could identify intrinsic factor(s) that predict lower exposure it may be possible to identify a subpopulation of patients that might benefit from increasing the dose from 12.5 mg to 25 mg.

Analysis of condition	The prevalence of hypertension in the United States is high, and nearly half of adults in the United States (approximately 48%) have a systolic blood pressure >130 mmHg, diastolic blood pressure >80 mmHg, or are taking a medication for hypertension. Hypertension is associated with an increased risk of cardiovascular disease, stroke, and end-stage kidney disease.
A continuous association exists between higher blood pressure and increased cardiovascular disease risk, and even modest reductions of severe hypertension can provide substantial benefit.
Hypertension that is difficult-to-control is often characterized as “uncontrolled” (i.e., blood pressure above goal) or “resistant” hypertension. Resistant hypertension is defined as blood pressure that remains above goal despite concurrent use of three antihypertensive agents of different classes (including a diuretic) at maximum recommended (or maximally tolerated) doses. The prevalence of true resistant hypertension is not known, and many patients who meet the definition of resistant hypertension have been found to have poor adherence or inadequate treatment regimens.
Hypertension is common in the United States and is a serious disease associated with significant morbidity and mortality. Patients with uncontrolled hypertension are at high risk for cardiovascular events.
Current treatment options	Numerous antihypertensive drugs from a variety of pharmacologic classes and with different mechanisms of action are available and have been shown in randomized controlled trials to reduce cardiovascular morbidity and mortality. From these trials, it can be concluded that it is the blood pressure reduction, and not some other pharmacologic property of the drugs, that is largely responsible for those benefits.
Many patients will require more than one drug from different pharmacological classes to achieve blood pressure goals. Patients with difficult-to-control hypertension often need several drugs (e.g., ≥3) from different pharmacological classes to achieve blood pressure goals.
Many patients will require more than one drug from different pharmacological classes to achieve blood pressure goals. Despite the availability of several classes of antihypertensive therapies, many patients remain uncontrolled.
L52: ## NDA 217686
## TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
L53: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Benefit	The efficacy of aprocitentan was evaluated in a multipart, Phase 3 multicenter study (PRECISION) in adults with SBP \ge140 mmHg who were prescribed at least three antihypertensive medications. The study evaluated the effect of aprocitentan 12.5 mg and 25 mg on blood pressure. The study included a 4-week double-blind, placebo-controlled treatment period (Part 1), 32-week single-blind treatment period (Part 2), and a 12-week placebo-controlled, double-blind withdrawal period (Part 3). The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline to Week 4 of double-blind treatment in mean trough SiSBP, recorded via uAOBPM. The key secondary endpoint was the change from double-blind-withdrawal baseline (Week 36) to Week 40 in mean trough SiSBP, recorded via uAOBPM.
In the PRECISION study, both the 12.5 mg and 25 mg doses met the primary endpoint. The LSM difference (97.5% CI) in SiSBP at Week 4 compared to baseline was -15.4 mmHg (-17.5, -13.3) for aprocitentan 12.5 mg, -15.2 mmHg (-17.3, -13.2) for aprocitentan 25 mg, and -11.6 mmHg (-13.7, -9.5) for placebo1, for a difference versus placebo of -3.8 mmHg (-6.8, -0.8; p-value=0.0043) and -3.7 mmHg (-6.6, -0.7; p-value=0.0054), respectively.
The PRECISION study also met the key secondary endpoint, demonstrating the persistence of aprocitentan's BP lowering effect. The LSM difference in blood pressure at Week 40 compared to Week 36 for aprocitentan 25 mg compared to placebo was -5.7 mmHg (95%CI: -7.8, -3.6; p-value<0.0001).
Aprocitentan's blood pressure lowering effect appeared consistent among subgroups defined by age, sex, race, BMI, baseline eGFR, baseline UACR, and medical history of diabetes.
The submitted data provide substantial evidence of aprocitentan's effectiveness in lowering blood pressure when administered in combination with other antihypertensive medications in patients who are not adequately controlled on other drugs.
Data from Part 1 of the PRECISION study indicate that in the study population as a whole, the 12.5 mg and 25 mg doses of aprocitentan provided similar efficacy and (b) (4)
the 12.5 mg dose will be approved.

Risk and risk management	Risks of aprocitentan in PRECISION were generally consistent with the known risks of endothelin receptor antagonists.
The results of safety analyses were as a whole consistent with risks that might be expected
L54: ## NDA 217686
## TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
L55: Dimension	Evidence and Uncertainties	Conclusions and Reasons
Anemia is a known risk of endothelin receptor antagonists and is thought to be due in part to hemodilution. In the initial 4-week double-blind treatment period, the incidence of a hemoglobin decrease >2 g/dL compared to baseline was greater in subjects receiving apocitentan (7% and 3% in the apocitentan 12.5 mg and 25 mg arms, respectively) compared to subjects treated with placebo (1%). Hemoglobin decreases usually presented early, stabilized thereafter, and were reversible after discontinuation.
Fluid retention is a known risk of endothelin receptor antagonists. In Part 1, fluid retention was reported in 9% of subjects receiving apocitentan 12.5 mg as compared with 18% of subjects receiving apocitentan 25 mg and 2% on placebo. The majority of edema events were reported as mild to moderate in severity and did not lead to treatment discontinuation.
Endothelin receptor antagonists can cause hepatotoxicity, though the degree of risk varies amongst members of the class. In PRECISION, there were two subjects with liver enzyme elevations of greater than 5-fold the upper limit of normal which were assessed as highly likely related to the use of apocitentan. Both subjects had positive rechallenges. There were no cases of ALT and/or AST >3 \times ULN and total bilirubin >2 \times ULN or liver failure.
Based on data from animal reproduction studies with endothelin receptor antagonists, apocitentan can cause fetal harm when administered during pregnancy.
Based on data from other endothelin receptor antagonists, apocitentan may have an adverse effect on spermatogenesis.
Hypersensitivity related-AEs were reported in 0.8% of subjects taking apocitentan 12.5 mg and 0.4% of subjects taking 25 mg compared with no patients receiving placebo in Part. During Part 2, one subject taking 25 mg had an SAE of allergic dermatitis requiring hospitalization and experienced recurrence of the allergic dermatitis with rechallenge.
given the known risks of macitentan and other endothelin receptor antagonists.
Because of the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity, apocitentan will only be available through a restricted distribution program under a REMS.
Some endothelin receptor antagonists can cause severe liver injury, including liver failure. Although the available data indicate that apocitentan can cause liver injury, the injuries seen in the apocitentan clinical trials were modest and reversible. Given these findings, as well as the existing risk mitigation for macitentan (apocitentan is a metabolite of macitentan), labeling is considered adequate to mitigate this risk, similar to macitentan. Postmarketing surveillance will include enhanced pharmacovigilance for liver injury to assess for serious events occurring in the postmarketing setting.
Labeling will contraindicate use in pregnancy and in patients who are hypersensitive to apocitentan or any of its excipients. Given the safety findings in the development program and larger experience with the pharmacologic class, labeling will also include Warnings and Precautions for embryo-fetal toxicity, hepatotoxicity, fluid retention, hemoglobin decrease, and decreased sperm counts.
L56: 1 The primary objective of the PRECISION study was to demonstrate the blood pressure-lowering effect of apocitentan when added to standard-of-care in subjects with “true” resistant hypertension. The substantial reduction in blood pressure seen in the placebo arm in Part 1 of the study indicates that while the study population had uncontrolled hypertension, it was not necessarily “true” resistant hypertension.

L58: ## 2.2. Conclusions Regarding Benefit-Risk
L59: Aprocitentan at doses of 12.5 mg daily and 25 mg daily was demonstrated to lower blood pressure in patients taking other antihypertensive medications, and thus is expected to reduce the risk of cardiovascular events in these patients. Although two doses were studied in the Phase 3 trial, the 25 mg dose does not appear to provide a meaningful improvement in blood pressure reduction as compared to the 12.5 mg dose.
L60: Based on data from animal reproduction studies with endothelin receptor antagonists, aprocitentan can cause embryo-fetal toxicity when administered during pregnancy and a REMS will be required to mitigate this risk by ensuring prescribers are educated about appropriate patient counseling (i.e., need for contraception and pregnancy testing). Although the available data indicate that aprocitentan can cause liver injury, the injuries seen in the aprocitentan clinical trials were modest and reversible. Similar to macitentan, hepatotoxicity will be included as a labeled risk for aprocitentan, and periodic liver tests will be recommended. Other risks (i.e., fluid retention, hemoglobin decrease, and decreased sperm counts) will also be described as Warnings in the label. To mitigate the risks of fluid retention and hemoglobin decrease, the label will also include periodic monitoring for signs and symptoms of fluid retention, weight gain, and worsening heart failure, and periodic assessments of hemoglobin, respectively.
L61: Given the measures that will be put in place, aprocitentan's benefits outweigh its risks. Because of aprocitentan's safety profile, as well as the availability of other antihypertensive agents with more benign safety profiles, the benefit-risk profile is considered favorable only when used in combination with other agents to lower blood pressure in patients who are not adequately controlled on other drugs (i.e., not as first-line therapy).1 For these patients, the clinical benefit of reducing cardiovascular risk outweighs risks that are either manageable (fluid retention, hemoglobin reduction, liver injury) or have rigorous risk mitigation requirements (embryofetal toxicity).

L63: the 12.5 mg dose will be approved.
L64: 1 Although we acknowledge members of the endothelin receptor antagonist class are indicated as first-line therapy for other diseases (e.g., pulmonary arterial hypertension), the benefit risk considerations are different for essential hypertension for which there are multiple other therapeutic options with more reassuring safety profiles.
L65: ## II. Interdisciplinary Assessment
L66: ## 3. Introduction
L67: Aprocitentan is an orally active, dual endothelin-receptor antagonist (ERA) that inhibits the binding of endothelin (ET)-1 to ET receptors A and B (ET_A and ET_B). ET-1, via its receptors (ET_A and ET_B), mediates a variety of deleterious effects such as vasoconstriction, fibrosis, cell proliferation and inflammation. ET-1 upregulation and can cause endothelial dysfunction, vascular hypertrophy and remodeling, sympathetic activation and increased aldosterone synthesis, and thus, contributes to blood pressure elevation. Aprocitentan has the same chemical structure as the active metabolite of macitentan. Macitentan is approved for the treatment of pulmonary arterial hypertension (PAH, WHO Group I) to reduce the risks of disease progression and hospitalization for PAH.
L68: On December 19, 2022, the Applicant submitted an NDA for aprocitentan for the treatment of hypertension to lower blood pressure under section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act.
L69: ## Disease Background
L70: The prevalence of hypertension in the United States is high,2 and nearly half of adults in the United States (approximately 48%) have a systolic blood pressure &gt;130 mmHg, diastolic blood pressure &gt;80 mmHg, or are taking a medication for hypertension (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 2023). In the United States, the prevalence of hypertension is higher among males, older adults, Black adults, and those with obesity, chronic kidney disease, or diabetes (Ostchega et al. 2020). Hypertension is associated with an increased risk of cardiovascular disease, stroke, and end-stage kidney disease. In 2010, in the United States, hypertension accounted for more cardiovascular deaths than any other modifiable cardiovascular disease risk factor and was second to cigarette smoking as a preventable cause of death for any reason (Whelton et al. 2018). A continuous association exists between higher blood pressure and increased cardiovascular disease risk, and even modest reductions of severe hypertension can provide substantial benefit.
L71: Treatment of hypertension is among the most common reasons for healthcare office visits and for the use of chronic prescription medications. Control of high blood pressure with medication is part of comprehensive cardiovascular risk management (e.g., lipid control, smoking cessation, exercise, and limited sodium intake). Numerous antihypertensive drugs, from a variety of pharmacologic classes and with different mechanisms of action are available and have been shown in randomized controlled trials to reduce cardiovascular morbidity and mortality. From these trials, it can be concluded that it is the blood pressure reduction, and not some other pharmacologic property of the drugs, that is largely responsible for those benefits. The largest
L72: 2 The exact prevalence of hypertension depends upon the definition of hypertension and varies according to the guideline/threshold used. Per the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey (NHANES) conducted between 2017-2018, in the United States, approximately 45% of U.S. adults met the definition of hypertension per the 2017 ACC/AHA guideline (i.e., BP &gt;130/80 mmHg).
L73: and most consistent cardiovascular outcome benefit has been a reduction in the risk of stroke, but reductions in myocardial infarction and cardiovascular mortality also have been seen regularly. Many patients will require more than one drug from different pharmacological classes to achieve blood pressure goals. Primary agents for the treatment of high blood pressure include thiazide diuretics, angiotensin converting enzyme inhibitors, angiotensin receptor blockers (ARBs), and calcium channel blockers. Secondary agents include loop diuretics, potassium-sparing diuretics, aldosterone antagonists, beta-blockers, direct renin inhibitors, alpha-1 blockers, and centrally acting drugs, and direct vasodilators (Whelton et al. 2018).
L74: Hypertension that is difficult-to-control is often characterized as “uncontrolled” (i.e., blood pressure above goal) or “resistant” hypertension. Resistant hypertension is defined as blood pressure that remains above goal despite concurrent use of three antihypertensive agents of different classes at maximum recommended (or maximally tolerated) doses. If tolerated, one of the three agents should be a diuretic. Patients whose blood pressure is controlled with four or more medications are also considered to have resistant hypertension. “True” resistant hypertension is distinguished from “apparent resistant” and pseudoresistant hypertension through 24-hour ambulatory blood pressure monitoring and lack of attribution to other factors (e.g., poor adherence to antihypertensive therapy, white coat hypertension, poor adherence to dietary and lifestyle approaches, suboptimal antihypertensive therapy, and inaccurate measurement of blood pressure). The prevalence of true resistant hypertension is not known, and many patients who meet the definition of resistant hypertension have been found to have poor adherence or inadequate treatment regimens. Data from the National Health and Nutrition Examination Survey from 2003 through 2008 in nonpregnant US adults with hypertension showed that approximately 9% met criteria for apparent resistant hypertension. Adults with resistant hypertension were more likely to be older, non-Hispanic black, have higher body mass index, albuminuria, reduced renal function, and self-reported medical histories of coronary heart disease, heart failure, stroke, and diabetes mellitus (Persell 2011).

L91: ## 5. Pharmacologic Activity, Pharmacokinetics, and Clinical Pharmacology
L92: ## 5.1. Nonclinical Assessment of Potential Effectiveness
L93: ## 5.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
L94: ET-1 mediates a variety of deleterious effects via activation of its receptors (ET_A and ET_B), such as vasoconstriction, fibrosis, proliferation, hypertrophy, and inflammation. Aprocitentan is an ERA that potently inhibits the binding of ET-1 to ET_A and ET_B receptors. Aprocitentan showed receptor inhibitor behavior both in vitro and in vivo, with a consistent preferential inhibition for ET_A versus ET_B receptors noted across studies.
L95: - The inhibitory effect of aprocitentan on receptor function was assessed using microsomal membranes from Chinese hamster ovary (CHO)-K1 cells expressing recombinant human ET_A or ET_B receptors. In this assay, aprocitentan inhibited ^{125}I-ET-1 binding to human recombinant ET_A and ET_B receptors with half maximal inhibitory concentration values of 3.4\pm 0.4 nM and 987\pm 185 nM, respectively. Therefore, inhibition of ET_A was approximately 290-fold more potent than for ET_B in this cell-free study.
- In recombinant cells, aprocitentan inhibited the ET-1-induced increase in cytosolic Ca^{2+} (a measure of downstream ET-1 signaling) with a geometric mean Schild K_b inhibitor potency value of 5.5 nM and 319 nM on ET_A and ET_B receptors, respectively, where K_b is the inhibition constant of the binding of the antagonist to the receptor. Thus, inhibition was approximately 60-fold more potent for ET_A than ET_B in intact cells.
- Aprocitentan inhibited ET-1-induced contraction (ET_A-mediated) in endothelium denuded rat aorta and sarafotoxic S6c-induced contraction (ET_B-mediated) in epithelium denuded rat trachea with pA2 values of 6.7\pm 0.2 and 5.5\pm 0.3, respectively. The pA2 value represents negative logarithm of the molar concentration of an antagonist that produces a 2-fold shift in the concentration response curve. These data demonstrated functional inhibitory activity of aprocitentan on ET_A or ET_B in isolated tissues.
- Given the difference in potency between ET_A and ET_B observed in vitro, a study was conducted to establish the inhibitory effect of aprocitentan on the ET_B receptor in vivo, which is reported to be involved in the clearance of ET-1 from the plasma (Löffler et al. 1993; Fukuroda et al. 1994). (Löffler et al. 1993; Fukuroda et al. 1994). Aprocitentan treatment in normotensive rats resulted in a dose-dependent increase in plasma ET-1 demonstrating its inhibitory effect on ET_B in vivo. These data indicate that while a greater potency for ET_A versus ET_B exists, as demonstrated in vitro, aprocitentan can also inhibit the ET_B receptor in vivo.
L96: ## 5.1.2. Animal Model Data Showing Proof of Concept for Efficacy
L97: Proof of concept supporting the efficacy of aprocitentan for the proposed indication was demonstrated in various animal models including deoxycorticosterone acetate (DOCA)-salt rats in which hypertension is induced by unilateral nephrectomy, mineralocorticoid analog DOCA pellet implantation and exposure to dietary salt (1% NaCl in drinking water); Dahl-S rats in which hypertension is induced by increased dietary salt (1% NaCl in drinking water); spontaneously hypertensive (SHR) rats in which hypertension is mediated through the renin angiotensin system and sympathetic nervous system.
L98: Acute administration of aprocitentan resulted in a dose-dependent decrease in blood pressure (BP) without any effect on heart rate in all three animal models, demonstrating an antihypertensive effect. At a dose of 100 mg/kg, aprocitentan decreased mean arterial pressure more effectively in Dahl-S (\downarrow 36 mmHg) and DOCA-salt (\downarrow 29 mmHg) rats than in SHR (\downarrow 18 mmHg), indicating that activation of the ET system was more prominent in salt-sensitive and volume-dependent hypertensive models (DOCA-salt and Dahl-S models) compared to the essential hypertension model (SHR rats).
L99: The protective effect of aprocitentan on heart and kidney function was evaluated in DOCA-salt rats following a 4-week treatment period. In DOCA-salt rats, aprocitentan dose-dependently attenuated mean arterial pressure (without affecting heart rate), increased renal blood flow, decreased renal vascular resistance, and reduced elevated plasma N-terminal pro-brain natriuretic peptide (NT-proBNP) levels. However, treatment did not fully ameliorate the cardiac (cardiomyopathic condition) and kidney (minimal to marked hydronephrosis, tubulonephrosis, interstitial nephritis and glomerular changes) findings. The results of this study suggest that aprocitentan treatment may partially improve cardiac and renal function in a hypertensive rat model.
L100: Treatment with aprocitentan in combination with other standard antihypertensive therapies was also evaluated in DOCA-salt rats and SHR rats. Aprocitentan, compared with spironolactone, was tested in combination with other standard antihypertensive therapies (e.g., valsartan, enalapril and amlodipine). The aprocitentan/valsartan combination demonstrated synergistic effect in DOCA-salt and SHR rats whereas the spironolactone/valsartan combination only produced additive effects in these models. The aprocitentan/enalapril combination demonstrated synergistic effect in SHR rats and an additive effect in DOCA-salt rats. The aprocitentan/amlodipine combination showed only additive effects in both rat models. These results suggested that aprocitentan treatment may have an additive or synergistic effect on BP reduction when coadministered with other antihypertensive drugs.
L101: ## 5.2. Clinical Pharmacology/Pharmacokinetics
L102: - No dose adjustment is required in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment. Aprocitentan has not been studied in patients with severe hepatic impairment (Section 8.1).
- No dose adjustment is needed for patients with renal impairment; however, aprocitentan was not studied in patients with eGFR &lt;15 mL/min or in patients undergoing dialysis and is not recommended in these patients (Section 8.1).
- Aprocitentan did not alter the pharmacokinetic (PK) profile of midazolam or 1-hydroxy midazolam to a clinically relevant extent (Section 8.2).
- Aprocitentan did not alter the PK of rosuvastatin to a clinically relevant extent (Section 8.2).
- Aprocitentan may be taken with or without food (Section 8.2).
- Glucosidation of aprocitentan to M3 accounts for 25.2% of the administered dose (Section 8.2).

Volume of distribution	19.3 L
Plasma protein binding	99.5% (in vitro)
Drug as substrate of transporters	Aprocitentan is a substrate of P-gp and BCRP in vitro. Inhibitors of these transporters are not expected to influence the PK of aprocitentan. Aprocitentan has a high permeability across cell membranes, so the cellular uptake is mainly driven by passive diffusion.
Elimination
Mass balance results	Urine was the primary route of elimination. A total of 52% of drug material was eliminated in the urine, and 25% was eliminated in feces. In the plasma, no drug metabolite was detected that exceeded 10% of drug-related exposure. The main metabolites in urine were M3, M6, M16, and A1. The main products in feces were M1 and unchanged aprocitentan. Two metabolic pathways contributed to ≥25% of 14C-radioactive elimination—glucosidation of aprocitentan to M3, and hydrolysis of aprocitentan to M1, which then undergoes other reactions leading to A1, A4, A5, M4, M5, M6, M11, M13, and M16.
Clearance	CL/F is approximately 0.3 L/h
Half-life	Terminal plasma half-life is approximately 46 hours, and effective half-life is approximately 41 hours
Metabolic pathway(s)	In the mass balance study, two metabolic pathways contributing to ≥25% of 14C-radioactive elimination were detected:Glucosidation of aprocitentan to M3 by UGT1A1 and UGT2B7 (accounts for approximately 25.2% of the administered dose)
Hydrolysis of aprocitentan to M1 which then undergoes several other reactions leading to A1, A4, A5, M4, M5, M6, M11, and M13 (accounts for approximately 31.5% of the administered dose). M1 is formed by both chemical and enzymatic hydrolysis. It is believed that the hydrolysis is mostly chemical rather than enzymatic.

Primary excretion pathways (% dose)	After a single oral dose of radiolabeled aprocitentan, approximately 52% of the dose was eliminated via urine (0.2% unchanged) and 25% (6.8% unchanged) via feces.
Intrinsic Factors and Specific Populations
Body weight	Based on popPK analysis, body weight does not affect PK to a clinically relevant extent (Section 14.5.2.5).
Age	Healthy elderly subjects (aged 65 to 80 years) Cmax: 1.3-fold and AUCr: 1.2-fold of that of healthy nonelderly subjects (aged 18 to 45 years)
Renal impairment	After a 50 mg dose of aprocitentan, compared to healthy subjects, the geometric mean ratio for Cmax was 1.04 in those with severe renal impairment. The AUC0-t and AUC0-∞ were 32% and 34% higher, respectively, in those with severe renal impairment compared to those with normal renal function.
Hepatic impairment	After a 25 mg dose of aprocitentan, compared to healthy subjects, the geometric mean ratios for Cmax, AUC0-t, and AUC0-∞ were 1.03, 1.2, and 1.2, respectively, in those with moderate hepatic impairment.

L111: ## 6. Efficacy (Evaluation of Benefit)
L112: ## 6.1. Assessment of Dose and Potential Effectiveness
L113: The Applicant has proposed a recommended (b) (4) dose of aprocitentan is 12.5 mg orally once daily, (b) (4) . The Applicant's proposed dose (b) (4) based on the Applicant's interpretation of the results of the Phase 2 dose-finding study (Study AC-080A201) and Phase 3 study (PRECISION). The Phase 3 study evaluated doses of 12.5 mg and 25 mg vs. placebo in a parallel arm design. These doses were selected based on
L114: the results of the Phase 2 dose-finding study and Phase 1 pharmacodynamic study (Study AC-080-102), which suggested that doses of aprocitentan in the range of 10 mg to 25 mg might provide the best benefit/risk profile. The phase 3 study also evaluated the 25 mg dose in a randomized withdrawal period.
L115: ## **Data Supporting Dose Selection for the Applicant's Phase 3 Study**
L116: In the Phase 2 dose-finding study, subjects with essential hypertension were randomized to receive aprocitentan (5, 10, 25, or 50 mg), 20 mg lisinopril, or placebo for 8 weeks. Aprocitentan doses of 10, 25, and 50 mg decreased sitting systolic/diastolic unattended automated office blood pressure measurement (uAOBPM) from baseline to Week 8, with placebo-corrected reductions of 7.1/4.9, 9.9/7.0, and 7.6/5.0 mmHg, respectively, compared with a reduction of 4.8/3.8 mmHg with lisinopril 20 mg. For those with valid ambulatory blood pressure monitor readings, aprocitentan 10, 25, and 50 mg significantly decreased placebo-corrected 24-hour systolic/diastolic blood pressure by 4.0/4.0, 4.8/5.9, and 3.7/4.5 mmHg, respectively. Based on the Phase 2 study results, the Applicant noted that the maximum effect on blood pressure appeared to correspond to the 25 mg dose, with similar effects on both uAOBPM and ambulatory blood pressure monitoring (ABPM) readings at the 10 mg and 50 mg doses. For additional discussion of the Phase 2 study results, see Section 14.2.
L117: The Applicant also conducted a Phase 1 study to better understand the relationship between the dose of aprocitentan and risk of fluid retention (as assessed by changes in body weight). In this Phase 1 study, healthy male subjects on a high sodium diet were randomized to placebo or aprocitentan doses of 10, 25, or 50 mg, and to one of two treatment sequences (A-B or B-A). Treatment A received aprocitentan once daily from Day 1 to Day 9, and Treatment B received placebo from Day 1 to Day 9. The 10 mg dose was considered to be “non-inferior” to placebo because the upper limit of the 90% confidence interval (CI) of body weight increase did not exceed 1 kg (mean increase of +0.4 kg, CI 0.05-0.8). In contrast, the 25 mg and 50 mg doses had an upper limit of the 90% CI that exceeded 1 kg (mean body weight increases of +0.8 kg, CI 0.03-1.5 and +0.8 kg, CI 0.3-1.3, respectively), suggesting a greater risk at the 25 mg as compared to the 10 mg dose. For additional discussion of the results of Study AC-080-102, see Section 14.2.
L118: ## **Findings in Applicant's Phase 3 Study**
L119: As discussed in Section 6.2, the Applicant's Phase 3 study was a multipart study in adults with a systolic blood pressure (SBP) \ge 140 mmHg (based on uAOBPM) who were prescribed at least three antihypertensive medications. In Part 1 of the study, 730 subjects were randomized 1:1:1 to aprocitentan 12.5 mg, aprocitentan 25 mg, or placebo once daily for a 4-week double-blind treatment period. The mean (\pm SE) change in SBP at 4 weeks was -15.3\pm 0.9 mmHg for aprocitentan 12.5 mg, -15.2\pm 0.9 mmHg for 25 mg, and -11.5\pm 0.9 mmHg for placebo, for a difference versus placebo of -3.8\pm 1.3 mmHg (97.5% CI: -6.8, -0.8; p=0.004) and -3.7\pm 1.3 mmHg (97.5% CI: -6.7, -0.8; p=0.005), respectively. The results of exploratory analyses based on other blood pressure parameters (e.g., mean sitting diastolic blood pressure [SiDBP]) measured by automated office blood pressure measurement (AOBPM), 24 hour mean SBP measured by ABPM, 24 hour mean diastolic blood pressure (DBP) measured by ABPM, daytime mean SBP measured by ABPM, daytime mean DBP measured by ABPM, night-time mean SBP
L120: measured by ABPM, and night-time mean DBP measured by ABPM) are shown in Table 6. Based on the Applicant's point estimates, the difference in BP response, if real, is modest.

L134: ## 6.2. Clinical Studies/Trials Intended to Demonstrate Efficacy
L135: ## 6.2.1. PRECISION Study
L136: ## 6.2.1.1. PRECISION Study Design
L137: ## Study Design
L138: The efficacy of aprocitentan was evaluated in a multipart, Phase 3 multicenter study (PRECISION) in adults with SBP \ge 140 mmHg who were prescribed at least three antihypertensive medications. The study included a placebo run-in period which was followed by three parts, as described below. Prior to the placebo run-in period, all subjects were switched to
L139: ## NDA 217686
## TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
L140: standard background antihypertensive therapy (SBAT) consisting of an ARB (valsartan, 160 mg), a calcium channel blocker (amlodipine, 5 or 10 mg), and a diuretic (hydrochlorothiazide, 25 mg). SBAT was continued throughout the study. Subjects with concomitant use of a stable dose of a beta blocker (any indication) that was initiated at least 4 weeks prior to the screening visit continued this treatment throughout the study.
L141: Following the 4-week placebo run-in period, 730 subjects (whose SiSBP (uAOBPM) remained \ge 140 mmHg) were randomized 1:1:1 to aprocitentan 12.5 mg, aprocitentan 25 mg, or placebo once daily for the initial 4-week double-blind (DB) treatment period (Part 1). At the end of 4 weeks, all subjects entered the single-blind treatment period (Part 2) where they received aprocitentan 25 mg once daily for 32 weeks. At the end of the 32 weeks, subjects were rerandomized to receive either aprocitentan 25 mg or placebo, once daily, during a 12-week DB-withdrawal (WD) period (Part 3) (Figure 2). All subjects then entered a 30-day safety follow-up period.
L142: **Figure 2. PRECISION Study Design**
L143: Source: PRECISION clinical study report
L144: Abbreviations: EOS, end of study; EOT, end of treatment; FU, follow-up; V, visit
L145: ## Objectives
L146: The primary objective was to demonstrate the BP-lowering effect of aprocitentan when added to standard-of-care in subjects with true resistant hypertension3 (RHT).
L147: The secondary objectives were: (1) to demonstrate that the effect of aprocitentan on BP is durable when added to standard-of-care in subjects with true RHT, and (2) to evaluate the long-term safety and tolerability of aprocitentan in subjects with true RHT during 48 weeks of treatment.
L148: 3 Resistant hypertension was defined as subjects who failed to achieve their recommended BP treatment target with maximum tolerated doses of an appropriate regimen of 3 antihypertensive drugs from different classes. Subjects with pseudoresistant hypertension were to be excluded from the study (e.g., inappropriate blood pressure measurement, white coat effect, insufficient efforts to optimize therapy, or poor adherence).
L149: ## Endpoints
L150: The primary efficacy endpoint was the change from baseline to Week 4 of double-blind treatment in mean trough SiSBP, recorded via uAOBPM. Baseline was defined as the last available measurement before the start of DB treatment.
L151: The key secondary efficacy endpoint was the change from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40 in mean trough SiSBP, recorded via uAOBPM.
L152: ## Unattended Automated Office Blood Pressure Measurements
L153: The blood pressure procedures used for assessments followed the American Heart Association guidelines/Canadian Education Program on Hypertension (Pickering et al. 2005; Daskalopoulou et al. 2012). Unattended automated office blood pressure was measured throughout the study with an automatic oscillometric sphygmomanometer which was provided to each site by the central blood pressure laboratory. At each study visit, blood pressure was measured noninvasively from the same arm by the provided AOBPM device, which recorded multiple sitting blood pressure readings with the subject resting undisturbed and unattended in a quiet place for five minutes. At each assessment, SiSBP and SiDBP were measured five times with a 1-minute time interval between them. After discarding the first reading, the blood pressure device/system generated the average of the remaining four readings for SiSBP and SiDBP. Blood pressure was measured at trough (i.e., before taking the study treatment and the standardized background antihypertensive therapy) at around the same clock time.
L154: The baseline uAOBPM was defined as the last available measurement before or on the day of randomization. The DB-WD baseline (Week 36) was defined as the last available measurement before or on the day of rerandomization.
L155: For additional details, including procedures for assessing other blood pressure parameters, see Section 15.

- Mean SiSBP \ge140 mmHg recorded via uAOBPM at the screening visit.

L171: ## Disease/Condition
L172: - Pregnant or lactating subjects.
- Apparent/pseudo RHT due to white coat effect, medical inertia, poor therapeutic adherence, or secondary causes of hypertension (not including obstructive sleep apnea).

The BP remeasurement could be during the screening visit (e.g., after at least 3 hours), or at the next planned visit, or at an unscheduled visit. The time of remeasurement was at the Investigator's discretion, taking into consideration the subject's medical history.
- Documented transient ischemic attack, stroke, unstable angina, or myocardial infarction within 6 months prior to screening.
- Clinically significant unstable cardiac disease at screening or in the past, in the opinion of the Investigator, e.g., uncontrolled symptomatic arrhythmia, atrial fibrillation, congestive heart failure NYHA stage II with relevant mitral valve insufficiency and/or aortic stenosis, congestive heart failure NYHA stage III or IV.

- Type 1 diabetes mellitus
- Subjects working night shifts7
- Known concomitant life-threatening disease with a life expectancy &lt;18 months after screening.

- Known hypersensitivity to apocitentan or drugs of the same class, or any of the excipients.
- Treatment with another investigational treatment within 3 months prior to screening.

L182: ## 6.2.1.3. Statistical Analysis Plan, PRECISION Study
L183: ## Analysis Sets
L184: Full Analysis Set (FAS): All subjects who were randomized and have a baseline SiSBP recorded via uAOBPM.
L185: Modified FAS: All subjects from the FAS who were rerandomized in the DB-WD part of the study and who have a DB-WD baseline SiSBP recorded via uAOBPM.
L186: ## Sample Size Calculation
L187: The Applicant planned the sample size based on the secondary endpoint to maintain at least 85% power for the DB-WD period (Part 3). At least 600 subjects were to be randomized and at least 380 subjects were expected to be re-randomized in the DB-WD period.
L188: Specifically, a sample size of 380 would provide 90% power to detect difference of 5 mmHg between apocitentan 25 mg and placebo at two-sided alpha of 0.05, assuming the within-group SD for the change from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40 in mean trough SiSBP as measured by uAOBPM was around 15 mmHg (based on Study AC-080A201).
L189: ## Primary Analysis
L190: The primary analysis was conducted on the FAS for the three treatment groups (aprocitentan 25 mg, aprocitentan 12.5 mg, and placebo). The primary endpoint, change from baseline to Week 4 (Part 1) in mean trough SiSBP measured by uAOBPM, was analyzed using a mixed-effects model for repeated measures (MMRM) analysis with factors for treatment group, visit and treatment by visit interaction, and covariates for baseline SiSBP and the interaction between baseline SiSBP and visit. An unstructured covariance matrix was used to account for the within subject correlation.
L191: ## Intercurrent Event/Missing Data Handling
L192: A treatment policy strategy was used to handle intercurrent events including “Premature discontinuation of DB treatment,” “the addition / dose increase of a diuretic,” and “use of rescue medication” in the primary and secondary analyses (i.e., all uAOBPM measurements, irrespective of any intercurrent event(s), were to be included in the analysis).
L193: Missing data were not to be imputed but were to be handled by a mixed model based on the assumption that the data were missing at random (MAR).
L194: ## Sensitivity Analyses
L195: The impact of deviations from the MAR assumption was to be assessed by the following sensitivity analyses:
L196: - Copy reference multiple imputation
- Jump to reference multiple imputation
- Tipping point analysis
L197: Alternative estimand for treatment discontinuation and rescue medication:
L198: - Hypothetical strategy (i.e., uAOBPM recordings obtained more than 1 day after premature discontinuation of double-blind treatment were excluded from the main analysis (i.e., considered missing). uAOBPM recordings obtained after the addition of a diuretic, or use of rescue medication, were also excluded from the analysis.)
L199: Additional sensitivity analysis:
L200: - The primary analysis was to be performed excluding site 4204 given data integrity considers at the site (as per internal audit report).
L201: ## Subgroup Analyses
L202: The following subgroups were prespecified:
L203: - Age at screening (years): 18 to &lt;65, 65 to &lt;75, and ≥75
- Sex: male, female
- Weight at baseline (kg): ≤100, &gt;100
L204: ## NDA 217686
## TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
L205: - Body mass index (BMI) at screening (\text{kg}/\text{m}^2): &lt;30, 30 to &lt;40, \ge 40
- Race: Black or African American, White, Asian, Other‘Other’ includes American Indian or Alaska Native, Native Hawaiian or other Pacific Islander, and Other. The ‘Other’ category was not considered for subgroup analyses due to the mix of races and small number of subjects limiting the ability (i.e., low power) to detect differences between treatment groups.
- Region: Asia/Australia, Europe, North America
- Urine albumin-to-creatinine ratio (UACR) at baseline (\text{mg}/\text{g}): &lt;30, 30 to 300, &gt;300
- Chronic kidney disease (CKD) stage at baseline (eGFR, \text{mL}/\text{min}/1.73 \text{ m}^2): Stage 1 or 2 (\text{eGFR} \ge 60), Stage 3 or 4 (\text{eGFR} 15 \text{ to } &lt;60)
- Diabetes (medical history): Yes/No
L206: Protocol Version 1/2 versus protocol Version 3 at randomization: compared to the prior versions of the protocol, in protocol Version 3 Amendment 2, diuretics were no longer required to be one of the three antihypertensive classes for enrollment in the study.
L207: Subgroups were to be analyzed using the same model as the primary analysis. Interaction p-values were obtained from adding subgroup by treatment and subgroup by treatment by visit interaction terms to the primary analysis model.
L208: ## Secondary Analysis
L209: The secondary analysis (at randomized withdrawal phase) was to be conducted on the modified FAS for the two treatment groups (aprocitentan 25 mg and placebo). The secondary endpoint, change from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40 in mean trough SiSBP recorded via uAOBPM, was analyzed using a mixed model with factors for stratum (i.e., randomized treatment in Part 1), treatment group, visit, and treatment by visit interaction, and covariates for DB-WD baseline SiSBP and the interaction between DB-WD baseline SiSBP and visit, and an unstructured covariance matrix.
L210: Similar sensitivity analyses as for the primary analysis were to be conducted.
L211: ## Testing Strategy
L212: Three null hypotheses were to be tested in the study. The first two were based on the primary endpoint in DB Part 1, the third hypothesis was based on the secondary endpoint at DB-WD Part 3 (Figure 3).
L213: - *First null hypothesis (H10):* there was no difference between aprocitentan 25 mg and placebo in the mean change from baseline to Week 4 in mean trough SiSBP in Part 1.
- *Second null hypothesis (H20):* there was no difference between aprocitentan 12.5 mg and placebo in the mean change from baseline to Week 4 in mean trough SiSBP in Part 1.
L214: - *Third null hypothesis (H_{30}):* there was no difference between aprocitentan 25 mg and placebo in the mean change from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40 in mean trough SiSBP in Part 3.
L215: The first two (H_{10} and H_{20}) were tested in parallel using the Bonferroni correction (each with a two-sided alpha of 0.025), and the third (H_{30}) was only to be tested if H_{10} or H_{20} was rejected.
L216: **Figure 3. Testing Strategy**
L217: Source: Applicant's statistical analysis plan
L218: Abbreviations: \alpha, level of significance; H_{10}, first null hypothesis; H_{20}, second null hypothesis; H_{30}, third null hypothesis; \mu, population mean; V, mean changes from baseline for each endpoint
L219: ## Statistical Analysis Plan Versions
L220: The study was conducted from June 18, 2018 (first subject, first visit) to April 25, 2022 (last subject, last visit). Version 1 of the statistical analysis plan (SAP) was finalized on April 4, 2019. The SAP was subsequently amended twice. Version 2 was finalized on September 23, 2021. In this amendment, the details of the reference based multiple imputation were added. Version 3 of the SAP (the latest version) was finalized on April 6, 2022. In this amendment, the primary estimand was changed from using a hypothetical strategy to a treatment policy strategy based on the Agency's feedback.
L221: The changes that were made to the SAP via these amendments do not raise concerns about the interpretability of the study results.
L222: ## **6.2.1.4. Results of Analyses, PRECISION Study**
L223: The section that follows provides an overview of patient disposition in the overall trial, followed by a discussion of Part 1 and Part 3.
L224: ## Overall Disposition
L225: In the PRECISION study, 1965 subjects were screened, and 730 subjects were randomized to aprocitentan 25 mg (N=243), aprocitentan 12.5 mg (N=243) and placebo (N=244). Table 7 shows the disposition of subjects (based on their assigned treatment group in Part 1). Overall, of the 730 randomized subjects, 153 (20.1%) discontinued study treatment and 68 (9.3%) discontinued the study at some point during any of the three parts of the study (Table 7). Over the course of the three parts of the study, the main reasons for study treatment discontinuation
L226: were “adverse event”, “withdrawal by subject” and “other.” The main reasons for study discontinuation were “withdrawal by subject”, “death” and “other.”

L231: ## Results of Part 1
L232: ## Demographic
L233: Baseline demographics in Part 1 were balanced among the treatment arms (Table 8). The median age was 63 years (range 24 to 84 years). At baseline, 44% of subjects were aged \ge65 years. Approximately 10% were Hispanic or Latino (Centers for Disease Control and Prevention 2023).8 The study was an international study and of those enrolled, approximately 32% were enrolled at sites in North America. Of those enrolled at U.S. sites, 37% were Black or African American.

L254: ## Analyses of Primary Endpoint
L255: The primary endpoint of change from baseline in SiSBP at Week 4 was analyzed using an MMRM model. Because of concerns with the Applicant's prespecified analytic approach (i.e., that the approach to handling missing data was not reasonable and was in violation of the intent-to-treat [ITT] principle), the review team used a different method to analyze the primary endpoint (see "Missing data handling and FDA recommended primary analysis" below for further discussion of this issue). Based on the primary analysis recommended by FDA, statistical significance was demonstrated at a two-sided alpha level of 0.025 for aprocitentan 12.5 mg versus placebo [least square mean (LSM) difference: -3.8 mmHg; 97.5% CI:-6.8, -0.8; p-value=0.0043], and for aprocitentan 25 mg versus placebo [LSM difference: -3.7 mmHg; 97.5% CI:-6.6, -0.7; p-value=0.0054] (Table 11). The results are similar to the Applicant's prespecified
L256: analysis. See Section 6.3.1 for a discussion of the clinical meaningfulness of the treatment effect for the two doses of aprocitentan evaluated.
L257: There was no planned statistical comparison of the two aprocitentan doses, but point estimates and confidence intervals for treatment effects were numerically similar for the 12.5 mg (low) dose and 25 mg (high) dose of aprocitentan.
L258: See Sections 6.1 and 6.3 for additional analyses and discussion regarding treatment effects on blood pressure for the 12.5 mg and 25 mg doses of aprocitentan.

L265: ## Missing Data Handling and FDA Recommended Primary Analysis
L266: Missing SiSBP data at baseline, Week 2 and Week 4 are shown in Table 12 by treatment arm. No subjects had missing baseline values. In the overall study population, there were moderate missing rates for uAOBPM measurements at Week 2 and Week 4 (10% and 7%, respectively). A total of 20 (3%) subjects were missing data at both Week 2 and Week 4.

L271: There are two concerns with how the prespecified primary analysis handled these missing data:
L272: - Since the missing data were handled directly by MMRM, the 20 (3%) subjects missing both Week 2 and Week 4 data were not included in the primary analyses given that no postbaseline data were collected. This violates the ITT principle and could potentially introduce bias even though the impact might be small given the low missing rate (3%).
- For subjects that were included in the MMRM model, all the missing data were imputed under the MAR assumption, which is not the approach recommended by the FDA. During product development, FDA had indicated to the Applicant that “MMRM assumes the data missing at random (MAR). Subjects who receive rescue medication, prematurely discontinue treatment or withdraw from the study are likely to be different from those who remain in the study on treatment.”
L273: To further explore and address the above missing data issues, subjects in the FAS were divided into four groups based on their missing data status after treatment discontinuation:
L274: - Group a: Subjects who completed study treatment through Part 1 and completed study in Part 1 with available primary endpoint data.
- Group b: Subjects who did not complete study treatment through Part 1 but completed study in Part 1 with available primary endpoint data, i.e., retrieved dropouts.
- Group c: Subjects who completed study treatment through Part 1, but with missing primary endpoint data.
- Group d: Others, i.e., subjects who did not complete study treatment through Part 1 and with missing primary endpoint data, or subjects who discontinued study in Part 1.
L275: A summary for subjects by their missing data status are shown in Table 13.

L280: In principle, a retrieved dropout multiple imputation is preferred to handle missing data due to loss of follow-up and study withdrawal. However, according to Table 13, there were not enough retrieved dropout subjects to support a reliable retrieved dropout multiple imputation (Table 13). After further consideration, the statistical team concluded that missing data should be handled in the primary analysis using a mixture of jump to reference multiple imputation based on MNAR and standard multiple imputation based on MAR. Specifically, under the treatment policy strategy to handle intercurrent events as specified in the SAP, missing primary endpoint data for subjects in group 1c were imputed based on subjects in group 1a with standard multiple imputation (MAR assumption) and missing data in group 1d were imputed with jump to reference multiple imputation. This approach follows the ITT principle by including all randomized subjects and handles missing data more appropriately when the missing data assumptions are unverifiable. Similar results were obtained as compared to the prespecified primary analysis.
L281: ## Sensitivity Analyses
L282: To further assess the robustness of the primary analysis results to missing data imputation assumptions, the scientific misconduct reported at study Site 4204, and intercurrent event handling, the following sensitivity analyses were conducted.
L283: - For the reference-based multiple imputation approaches, copy reference and jump to reference multiple imputations were performed respectively. As opposed to the primary analysis, all subjects were included in the MMRM.
- For Site 4204, scientific misconduct, which led to subject participation in 2 clinical studies concurrently and serious violation of ALCOA+ (Principles of data integrity: attributable, legible, contemporaneous, original, accurate, complete, consistent, enduring, available) requirements for study site study documentation was identified. Therefore, a sensitivity analysis was conducted excluding all 27 randomized subjects from Site 4204.
- Since the primary analysis used a treatment policy strategy for treatment discontinuation and rescue medication, a supplementary analysis was performed using a hypothetical strategy for rescue medication, in which any data after the intercurrent event of rescue medication were ignored and then imputed using jump to reference multiple imputation.
L284: Results of the sensitivity/supplementary analyses showed similar treatment effects as compared to the primary analyses, and the p-values are all smaller than 0.025, indicating that the primary analyses results are robust (Table 14).

L289: ## Subgroup Analyses
L290: Subgroups analyses were conducted using the same model as used in the primary analyses. Treatment effects were consistent across prespecified subgroups for both dose arms, except for the UACR and CKD subgroups, for which interaction p-values indicated potential heterogeneity (interaction p-value of 0.0011 and 0.0019, respectively) (Figure 4 and Figure 5).
L291: For baseline UACR categories (&lt;30 mg/g, 30-300 mg/g, &gt;300 mg/g), the estimated treatment effects for the UACR &lt;30 mg/g subgroup and UACR =30-300 mg/g subgroup were similar to the overall treatment effects for both doses, while the estimated treatment effect for the UACR &gt;300 mg/g subgroup appeared to be larger with wider confidence interval, visually deviating from the other two subgroups for both doses relative to placebo. Given the small sample size of the UACR &gt;300 subgroup and that the treatment effect favored aprocitentan (Table 9), no further analyses were warranted.
L292: For baseline eGFR categories the estimated treatment effect appeared to be smaller than the overall treatment effect in subjects with eGFR&gt;=60 mL/min /1.73 m2 and greater than the overall treatment effect in subjects with eGFR 15-60 mL/min /1.73 m2. This finding was particularly notable for the 25 mg dose of aprocitentan, i.e., the confidence intervals of the treatment effects for the two CKD subgroups did not overlap and the estimated treatment effect for the subgroup with eGFR&gt;=60 mL/min /1.73 m2 was much smaller (LSM difference = -1.26) than the overall treatment effect. Additional subgroup analyses were conducted for baseline eGFR categories using different blood pressure endpoints (including uAOBPM SiDBP, 24h ABPM SiSBP and SiDBP) in Part 1 and data from Part 3. The results (see Section 16) suggest that the findings for the primary efficacy endpoint in eGFR subgroups may be due to chance and not reflect a true difference.
L293: ## Shrinkage Estimates
L294: There are typically some random highs and random lows in sample estimates of subgroup treatment effects due to small sample sizes and large variability for some subgroups. Therefore, we also derived shrinkage estimates of subgroup treatment effects using a Bayesian hierarchical
L295: ## NDA 217686
## TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
L296: model based on summary sample estimates. This approach leads to improved precision and lower variability, shown with narrower confidence intervals, most notably in subgroups with a smaller sample size. The total variability in the sample estimates is the sum of the within subgroup variability of the sample estimator and the across subgroups variability in the underlying/true parameter values. A shrinkage estimate of the subgroup treatment effect, which borrows information from the other subgroups while estimating the treatment effect for a specific subgroup, is a “weighted” average of the sample estimate and overall estimate. A set of fairly noninfluential priors [\mu \sim \text{Normal}(0, 10^2), \tau^2 \sim \text{HalfNormal}(0, 10^2) with lower limit of 10^{-12}] were used to derive shrinkage estimates for all subgroups. These results showed narrower confidence intervals and more consistent results across different subgroups.

L312: ## Summary of Findings in Part 1
L313: Part 1 assessed the efficacy of two doses of aprocitentan in reducing SiSBP using uAOBPM obtained at trough. Both doses were statistically superior to placebo in reducing SiSBP at Week 4. The size of the placebo-corrected treatment effect, which was similar in the 12.5 mg and 25 mg dose arms (a reduction of 3.8 mmHg and 3.7 mmHg, respectively), is considered clinically meaningful. Sensitivity analyses confirmed the robustness of the primary analysis results. For the most part, treatment effects were consistent across prespecified subgroups for both dose arms for the prespecified primary endpoint. However, for the UACR and eGFR subgroups, interaction p-values indicated potential heterogeneity. In addition, although the interaction p-value for the race variable did not suggest heterogeneity among race subgroups, the subgroup analyses showed neutral effects for Black or African American subjects for both aprocitentan 12.5 mg and 25 mg. Further analyses were conducted to explore these findings. These additional analyses suggested that the extreme large or small treatment effects observed in these subgroups were due to small sample sizes and randomness.
L314: ## Results of Part 3
L315: Part 3 of the study consisted of a 12-week double-blind period in which subjects were rerandomized to placebo or remained on aprocitentan. Subjects that completed treatment through Parts 1 and 2 were then rerandomized to aprocitentan 25 mg or placebo in Part 3. Of the 730 subjects randomized in Part 1, there were 614 subjects randomized to Part 3. This is more than the planned sample size of 380, the Applicant stated that the larger-than-planned sample size was caused by the addition of sites and countries in 2020. These additional sites and countries were meant to compensate for the lower recruitment due to the Covid-19 pandemic.
L316: ## Demographic
L317: Baseline demographics for Part 3 were generally balanced among the treatment arms (Table 17). A greater proportion of subjects &lt;65 years of age were randomized to the placebo group; however, the mean and median age was similar in the two arms. Overall, the demographics of the subjects who were rerandomized in Part 3 were similar to those of subjects in Part 1 (Table 8).

L330: ## Part 3 Disposition
L331: Of the 614 rerandomized subjects, 36 (6%) discontinued study drug during Part 3, and 22 (3.6%) discontinued the study (Table 19). The most frequently reported reasons for treatment discontinuation were adverse event [15 (2.4%)] and “other” [15 (2.4%)]. There was one death in the aprocitentan 25 mg group due to multiple organ dysfunction syndrome after the Week 40 analysis visit.

L344: ## Missing Data Handling and FDA Recommended Secondary Analysis
L345: Similar to the primary endpoint analysis, missing data were handled by MMRM in the prespecified main analysis of the secondary endpoint and assumed to be missing at random. At Week 40, 46 (15%) and 40 (13%) subjects were missing SiSBP measurements in the aprocitentan 25 mg group and placebo group, respectively, resulting in a 14% overall missing rate for Week 40 (Table 21). There were 22 subjects (4%) missing both Week 38 and Week 40 data. These 22 subjects were not included in the MMRM analysis for the secondary efficacy endpoint, raising the same concerns as those discussed for the primary analysis. As such, the FDA conducted an alternative analysis using similar methods as those used in the FDA analysis of the primary endpoint (i.e., a mixture of standard MI and J2R-MI) to obtain an estimate of the treatment effect in Part 3 that more appropriately handles missing data (see Table 20).

L350: Sensitivity analyses similar to those performed for the primary endpoint were also performed for the secondary endpoint (change in SiSBP from DB-WD baseline (Week 36) to Week 40). One additional sensitivity analysis was performed because of the high missing rate at Week 40. The main reason for the high missing rate (14%) at Week 40 was that 62 subjects performed the Week 40 visits outside of the Week 40 analysis window (Day 21 to Day 41 after rerandomization). The main analysis used actual analysis visits and considered these Week 40 visits as missing. Thus, a supplementary analysis was performed based on visits as per protocol (not analysis windows).
L351: The results of these sensitivity analyses are shown in Table 22. As shown in the table, the LSM differences for all of the sensitivity/supplementary analyses (as well as the main analysis result in Table 20) were larger than the LSM differences seen in Part 1. Because the actual sample size of 614 was much larger than the planned sample size of 380, the study was overpowered to detect aprocitentan's effect on blood pressure during the randomized withdrawal phase of the trial (assumed in power calculations to be 5 mmHg), which likely contributed to the high statistical significance of the p-values shown in the table. However, given that the estimated treatment effect during the randomized withdrawal phase was larger than in Part 1, as well as the results of the sensitivity/supplementary analyses, the secondary endpoint findings are considered to be robust and to support the conclusion that aprocitentan's blood pressure lowering effect is durable.

L356: ## Subgroup Analyses
L357: Subgroup analyses, including shrinkage analyses (where the same set of noninfluential priors as for the subgroup analyses of primary endpoint were used), were also performed for the secondary endpoint. Overall, the treatment effects were consistent across subgroups for the secondary endpoint (Figure 6). The Hispanic or Latino subgroup showed a treatment effect in the unfavorable direction, however, given the small sample size (and thus wide confidence interval) of this subgroup and the large treatment effect shown in Part 1 for apocitentan 25 mg versus placebo, it is likely that this unfavorable effect is not a real finding and reflects a random low value.

L360: ## Summary of Findings in Part 3
L361: The persistence of the BP-lowering effect of apocitentan was demonstrated in Part 3 of the study, with increased mean SiSBP in subjects rerandomized to placebo, and maintained mean SiSBP in subjects rerandomized to apocitentan 25 mg. The robustness of the main analysis was supported by the sensitivity analyses. Subgroup findings were, as a whole, consistent with the findings in the overall population.
