L363: ## 7. Safety (Risk and Risk Management)
L364: ## 7.1. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Nonclinical Data
L365: Nonclinical studies, including safety pharmacology studies, general toxicology studies, a full battery of genetic toxicology studies, and a partial battery of developmental and reproductive toxicology studies were conducted with aprocitentan. The Applicant also provided a letter of authorization referencing relevant data submitted to NDA 214410 for macitentan. Key information on the reproductive safety of aprocitentan was provided by the developmental and reproductive toxicology studies conducted previously with macitentan. Similarly, the carcinogenicity assessment for aprocitentan was based on studies conducted with macitentan. This approach was previously agreed upon between the Applicant and the Agency since
L366: aprocitentan is a major active metabolite of macitentan and represents 50% and approximately 90% of the combined exposure to macitentan and aprocitentan in rats and rabbits, respectively.
L367: The key safety findings from nonclinical studies and their potential clinical relevance are discussed below. The exposure multiples in the discussion that follows were calculated based on the AUC (34.8 µg·hr/mL) at the maximum recommended human dose (MRHD) of 12.5 mg/day. For a more detailed summary of nonclinical study, refer to Section 13.
L368: ## **Embryo-Fetal Toxicities**
L369: In embryo-fetal developmental (EFD) toxicity studies, treatment with macitentan (for which aprocitentan is a major metabolite) in pregnant rats and rabbits revealed teratogenicity and developmental toxicity at all doses tested, and therefore a no observed adverse effect level (NOAEL) for embryo fetal toxicity in the rat and rabbits could not be established. These findings were consistent with findings with other members of the ERA class. The relevant animal data are summarized below.
L370: - In pregnant rats, oral administration of 3, 10, and 150 mg/kg/day macitentan during the period of major organogenesis resulted in fetal abnormalities, which included cardiovascular and mandibular arch fusion malformations, at all doses tested. The aprocitentan AUC at the lowest macitentan dose tested (3 mg/kg/day) was approximately equivalent to the clinical exposure at the MRHD of 12.5 mg/day, based on AUC.
- In pregnant rabbits, oral administration of 2.5, 12.5, and 25 mg/kg/day macitentan during the period of major organogenesis resulted in fetal abnormalities, which included cardiovascular and mandibular arch fusion malformations, at all doses tested. The aprocitentan exposure at lowest dose tested (2.5 mg/kg/day) was approximately 15-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC.
- In a pre- and postnatal developmental study in female rats, oral administration of 10, 50 or 250 mg/kg/day macitentan during the pregnancy and the lactational periods reduced pup survival and impaired male fertility of offspring at all doses. The aprocitentan exposure at the lowest dose (10 mg/kg/day) was approximately 2-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC.
L371: Following submission of the aprocitentan NDA, the Applicant submitted additional EFD toxicity studies in which aprocitentan itself was administered to animals. No embryo-fetal toxicity was observed in the EFD studies with aprocitentan alone at 5-fold and 29-fold the human exposure at the MRHD in rats and rabbits, respectively. The highest dose of aprocitentan tested in the rat EFD study was too low to identify a lowest-observed adverse effect level, a critical attribute of an EFD study for a drug with a teratogenic mechanism of action. Indeed, the rat study tested doses only up to 5-fold the clinical exposure, based on AUC. The mechanism of action for ERAs is well established to be teratogenic in animals. In the absence of direct comparisons to other ERAs at their relevant clinical exposures, the available information is insufficient to conclude that [REDACTED] (b)(4). In conclusion, without studies that produced exposures resulting in the expected embryo-fetal toxicities, and without inclusion of a macitentan (or other ERA) comparator, the available data are insufficient to characterize the embryofetal risk of aprocitentan. Therefore, the risk assessment based on the
L372: studies conducted with macitentan continues to represent the most appropriate data for communicating the reproductive risk associated with aprocitentan. Reproductive risk could be reevaluated when adequately designed EFD studies with aprocitentan that include macitentan or other ERA as a comparator are provided by the Applicant.
L373: ## Fertility
L374: ## **Male Fertility**
L375: In a fertility study in male rats given aprocitentan for 15 weeks (including 10-week premating period, 2-week mating period and 3-week post-mating period) at doses up to 250 mg/kg/day (approximately 52-fold the exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC), no aprocitentan related effects on male gonadal function and mating behavior were observed. Likewise, no significant aprocitentan-related adverse effects on reproductive endpoints, including testicular and epididymal sperm count, sperm motility or morphology parameters, were observed. Minimal dilation of the seminiferous tubules, but not tubular degeneration or atrophy, was observed in the testes at \ge 50 mg/kg/day, which represents approximately 13-fold the exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
L376: In the repeat-dose toxicology studies in male rats and dogs given aprocitentan for 26 weeks or 39 weeks, respectively, at doses up to 250 mg/kg/day, an increased incidence of tubular degeneration/atrophy was observed at 250 mg/kg/day in rats and at \ge 25 mg/kg/day in dogs, representing approximately 41-fold and 53-fold, respectively, the exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC. The tubular findings in the testes were resolved at the end of the recovery period in both species. Reversible minimal dilation of seminiferous tubules of the testes were observed at doses \ge 50 mg/kg/day in rats and \ge 5 mg/kg/day in dogs. These findings were not associated with degeneration or atrophy and were not considered adverse. The NOAEL was thus established at 50 mg/kg/day in rats and 5 mg/kg/day in dogs, which represent approximately 14- and 11-fold, respectively, the exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
L377: In animal studies with several other ERAs, including bosentan, ambrisentan and macitentan (Actelion Pharmaceuticals US 2001; Gilead Sciences 2007; Actelion Pharmaceuticals US 2013), testicular tubular atrophy was observed in rodents, similar to what was observed with aprocitentan. Adverse effects on sperm count and male fertility were reported in rats treated with ambrisentan at high exposures, but similar findings were not reported with macitentan (Gilead Sciences 2007; Actelion Pharmaceuticals US 2013). Decreased sperm counts were reported in patients receiving bosentan (Actelion Pharmaceuticals US 2001), but clinical sperm evaluations are not available for ambrisentan and macitentan. Given that the testicular tubular degeneration/atrophy observed in rats and dogs treated with aprocitentan occurred at high exposures (&gt;41-fold of aprocitentan exposure at the MRHD) and were not seen at or below the tested doses that provided 14- or 11-fold aprocitentan exposure at the MRHD, the concern for potential clinical risk might typically be deemed to be low. However, given the clinical risk identified with bosentan, a potential for clinical fertility risk with aprocitentan in males cannot be excluded.
L378: ## **Female Fertility**
L379: In a fertility and early embryonic development study in female rats given apocitentan prior to mating, no effect on gonadal function, mating behavior, or fertility index was observed at doses up to 250 mg/kg/day. A higher percentage of acyclic females or females with an increased mean estrus cycle length was observed at 250 mg/kg/day. Mean number of implantation sites and mean number of corpora lutea was slightly reduced at \ge 50 mg/kg/day, which is approximately 23-fold the AUC at the MRHD for females, based on AUC. The NOAEL for all measured parameters was determined to be 10 mg/kg/day, which represents 5-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD, based on AUC. Given that no impairment of fertility function was observed, these findings are deemed to have limited clinical relevance.
L380: ## **Other Target Organ Toxicity**
L381: General toxicity studies were conducted for durations up to 26 weeks in rats and 39 weeks in dogs. Target organs of toxicity and the clinical relevance of the findings are discussed below.
L382: ## **Liver**
L383: In the 26-week toxicity study rats, a dose-dependent increase in liver weight and increasing incidence/severity of centrlobular hepatocellular hypertrophy and centrlobular fatty change (with a dark-colored appearance upon gross examination) were observed, which is considered an adaptive response caused by increased microsomal cytochrome P450 enzyme induction. At 250 mg/kg/day, approximately 41-fold the AUC at the MRHD, a moderate hepatocellular vacuolar degeneration/necrosis in the hypertrophic hepatocytes with minimal multinucleated hepatocytes was observed. In the 39-week toxicity study in dogs, an increased incidence of hepatocellular hypertrophy was also observed at \ge 25 mg/kg/day (approximately 53-fold the AUC at the MRHD). No degenerative changes were observed in dogs at any dose, including the 50 mg/kg/day high dose. No adverse liver changes were observed in rats and dogs at 50 mg/kg/day (approximately 14-fold the AUC at the MRHD) and 75/50 mg/kg/day (approximately 82-fold the AUC at the MRHD), respectively. Hepatotoxicity has been reported with other ERAs and was seen in clinical studies with apocitentan.
L384: ## **Hematological Toxicity**
L385: Treatment-related, reversible decreases in hemoglobin, RBC, and hematocrit (packed cell volume) were observed in female dogs at 75/50 mg/kg/day without any hemolysis, myelosuppression, or reticulocytes changes. No hematological changes were observed at 25 mg/kg/day, which is approximately 53-fold the AUC at the MRHD. While the large exposure margins and reversibility typically indicate minimal human risk, anemia has been reported with other ERAs and was seen in clinical studies with apocitentan.
L386: ## **Nasal Congestion**
L387: Reversible increases in the incidence/severity of nasal congestion associated minimal to moderate submucosal edema and Goblet cell proliferation were observed at \ge 25 mg/kg/day in dogs. These doses represent \ge 53-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC. Nasal congestion was also noted in the clinical studies at supra-pharmacological doses (&gt; 25 mg/kg/day). Given the foregoing, the human risk is considered to be low.
L388: ## Genetic Toxicology
L389: There was no evidence of mutagenicity or clastogenicity for aprocitentan in a standard battery of tests that included in vitro bacterial reverse mutation and chromosomal aberration assays, and an in vivo rat micronucleus study.
L390: ## Carcinogenicity
L391: The carcinogenicity assessment of aprocitentan was based on carcinogenicity studies conducted with another ERA, macitentan, for which its major metabolite is aprocitentan. No carcinogenicity studies were conducted with aprocitentan alone. Two-year carcinogenicity studies with macitentan did not identify any carcinogenic potential at doses up to 100 mg/kg/day and 250 mg/kg/day in mice and rats, where aprocitentan exposures represented approximately 30-fold and 11-fold, respectively, the clinical aprocitentan exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
L392: ## 7.2. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Based on Drug Class or Other Drug-Specific Factors
L393: Aprocitentan is an ERA. Risks described in labeling for approved ERAs include embryo-fetal toxicity, hepatotoxicity, pulmonary edema with pulmonary veno-occlusive disease, decreased sperm counts, decreases in hemoglobin and hematocrit, and fluid retention. To date, all ERAs have been approved with a risk evaluation and mitigation strategy (REMS) for embryo-fetal toxicity, and two of the ERAs, bosentan and sparsentan, also include risk mitigation for hepatotoxicity in the REMS.
L394: ## 7.3. Potential Risks or Safety Concerns Identified Through Postmarket Experience
L395: Aprocitentan is neither approved nor marketed in the United States or worldwide.
L396: ## 7.4. FDA Approach to the Safety Review
L397: The safety review included a review of data quality, as well as adverse events (AEs), laboratory data, and vital sign datasets. AEs were analyzed by the Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities (MedDRA) [version 24.1] preferred term and by pooling similar AEs (referred to as the MedDRA SMQ or Food and Drug Administration Medical Dictionary for Regulatory Activities query [FMQ]). The review team did not identify any major data quality or integrity issues that raised concern. No major issues were identified with respect to recording, coding, and categorizing AEs, and the translation of verbatim terms to MedDRA preferred terms for the AEs reported in PRECISION was found to be acceptable.
L398: The FDA safety review focused on the data collected in the PRECISION study. PRECISION included three treatment periods (Part 1, 2, and 3) with differing designs and durations, thus, the three treatment periods were analyzed separately. Adverse events were collected up through the
L399: safety follow-up period, which started on the day after the last dose of study treatment and ended with a safety follow-up visit 30–33 days later. Adverse events of special interest (AESIs) included treatment-emergent adverse events (TEAEs) suggestive of anemia, edema/fluid retention, decompensation of heart failure, and hepatic injury. Since the target population has multiple cardio-cerebrovascular risk factors, an additional safety topic of interest included treatment-emergent major adverse cardiovascular events (MACE), defined as cardiovascular death, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or nonfatal stroke, and MACE-plus, defined as hospitalization for heart failure, which were adjudicated by an independent central adjudication committee (CAC).
L400: Safety data for the initial treatment period (Part 1; 4-week placebo-controlled) are presented in the following sections. For AESIs and any TEAEs that warranted further evaluation, data from study Parts 2 and 3 are also presented if pertinent.
L401: ## 7.5. Adequacy of the Clinical Safety Database
L402: In the PRECISION study safety population, 724 subjects received at least one dose of aprocitentan, and the mean duration of exposure was 36.6 weeks (Table 24). Total subject exposures were sufficient to assess the safety of aprocitentan for the proposed indication and dose.

L412: ## 7.6. Safety Results
L413: ## 7.6.1. Overview of Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L414: In Part 1 of the PRECISION study, TEAEs and serious AEs (SAEs) were reported in a higher proportion of aprocitentan-treated subjects than placebo-treated (Table 25). AEs leading to permanent discontinuation were also reported in a slightly numerically greater proportion of aprocitentan- as compared to placebo-treated subjects. Although AEs leading to dose interruption were reported in a slightly numerically greater proportion of subjects in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg dose group as compared to placebo, a similar finding was not seen at the higher dose.

L424: ## 7.6.2. Deaths
L425: There were 11 treatment-emergent deaths (1.3%) during the study, all reported in aprocitentan-treated subjects. Of these deaths, one was reported in Part 1, nine were reported during Part 2 and one was reported in Part 3 (Table 26). Five of the deaths were attributed to COVID-19 (Part 1, n=1; Part 2, n=3; Part 3, n=1), one death was attributed to a colon perforation from a colonoscopy (Part 2), and one death was attributed to invasive breast carcinoma (primary cause of death) with concurrent subdural hemorrhage and brain edema (Part 2). The remaining four deaths were adjudicated as MACE, all reported in Part 2 with no comparator arm. Based on a review of the cases as well as what is known about the pharmacologic class, it seems unlikely that aprocitentan played a role in these deaths. See Section 7.6.6 for further discussion of MACE. Table 139 provides the listing of subject deaths; the narratives for the deaths adjudicated as MACE are provided in Table 140.

L433: ## 7.6.3. Serious Treatment-Emergent Adverse Events
L434: In Part 1 of PRECISION, SAEs were uncommon but reported in a slightly higher proportion of aprocitentan-treated subjects (3% in each dosing arm) as compared to placebo (1%). L446: ## 7.6.4. Adverse Events and FDA Medical Queries Leading to Treatment Discontinuation
L447: AEs leading to permanent discontinuation of study treatment were uncommon in Part 1 of the study but reported in a slightly higher proportion of aprocitentan-treated patients (3% and 2%, respectively, for the 12.5 mg and 25 mg dose) as compared to placebo (1%; Table 29). In Part 2, AEs leading to treatment discontinuation were reported in 3.8% of subjects; the proportion was slightly higher among subjects who switched from the placebo arm (in Part 1) (5.1%) than among subjects who switched from the 12.5 mg arm (2.6%) or remained on the 25 mg dose (3.8%; Table 146). After review of the cases, AEs leading to discontinuation due to edema, elevated transaminases, and hypersensitivity appear to be related to aprocitentan. See Sections 7.6.5.1, 7.6.5.3, and 7.6.5.4 for further discussion of these events, respectively. AEs resulting in discontinuation that were adjudicated as MACE are discussed in Section 7.6.6 and narratives are provided in Table 144.

L475: ## 7.6.5.1. Edema/Fluid Retention
L476: Fluid retention is a known risk of ERAs. Preferred terms for edema-related AEs were pooled via grouped query and included fluid retention, oedema peripheral, face oedema, pulmonary oedema, and localised oedema. In Part 1 of the PRECISION study, an imbalance in edema (grouped query) was observed between the apocitentan 12.5 mg and 25 mg arms (9% and 18%, respectively) and placebo arm (2%) (Table 31). There appeared to be a dose-response relationship. Time to onset was as early as the first week of treatment but continued throughout the study (Figure 7). In Part 2, edema AEs were reported in 17% of subjects (Table 31); the incidence was higher among subjects who switched from the placebo arm (23%) as compared to those subjects who switched from the 12.5 mg arm (16%) and those who remained on the 25 mg dose (12%; Table 143). SAEs of pulmonary edema were reported in one subject in the apocitentan 25 mg arm in Part 1 and one subject in Part 2; narratives are provided below. Both SAEs of pulmonary edema were reported in the context of significantly elevated blood pressure that most likely precipitated the events.
L477: Overall, the majority of edema events were reported as mild to moderate in severity, with six subjects discontinuing apocitentan due to edema (Table 31). For information on changes in body weight, see Section 7.6.9.1. Subgroup analyses for Part 1 suggested a greater risk in subjects greater than 65 years old, in subjects taking amlodipine 10 mg as background therapy, and in subjects with lower levels of kidney function (Table 142).

L489: ## Edema SAEs
L490: - Subject (b) (6) was a 67-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who had a SAE of pulmonary edema, cardiac failure, and increased blood pressure on Study Day 8-12. The subject was admitted to the hospital with a diagnosis of heart failure with preserved ejection fraction with flash pulmonary edema after experiencing increasing dyspnea. Blood pressure upon arrival was 220/104 mmHg and chest X-ray showed early pulmonary edema. The subject's NT-proBNP value during hospitalization was not reported. The subject recovered after treatment (blood pressure upon hospital discharge was 123/65 mm/Hg) and the event was considered not related to the study drug. There was no action taken with study treatment. On Study Day 23, the subject presented to the emergency department with severe chest pain and peripheral edema that resolved after treatment with transdermal nitroglycerin and furosemide. There was no action taken with study treatment and the event was considered not related to the study drug. Prior to the study (at screening), the subject's individual background antihypertensive therapy included valsartan 300 mg and nifedipine 60 mg which were switched to SBAT per study protocol. Blood pressure at baseline was 150/82 mmHg.
L491: - Subject (b)(6) was a 72-year-old Black male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who experienced an SAE of pulmonary edema, congestive heart failure, and hypertensive urgency on Study Day 134 to 137. The subject was admitted to the hospital with a diagnosis of congestive heart failure, pulmonary edema in the setting of hypertensive urgency, acute kidney injury on chronic kidney disease, and atrial flutter. Blood pressure upon arrival was reported as 209/60 mmHg and chest X-ray showed enlarged cardiac silhouette and pulmonary vasculature congestion. The subject's NT-proBNP value during hospitalization was not reported. The subject recovered after treatment with intravenous furosemide, nitrates, and hydralazine. Study treatment was interrupted due to the hypertension. The event was considered not related to study drug. Prior to the study (at screening), the subject's individual background antihypertensive therapy included SBAT and hydralazine (50 mg twice daily) which was discontinued at that time, per study protocol. The subject was restarted on study treatment on Study Day 147 and completed study Parts 2 and 3 in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm as scheduled.
L492: ## Adjudicated MACE-Plus/Heart Failure Hospitalization
L493: MACE-plus was defined as hospitalization for heart failure and adjudicated by the CAC. Over the course of the PRECISION study, eleven subjects experienced heart failure hospitalization adjudicated as MACE-plus, two of which were reported in Part 1 in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm (Table 32). One of the cases in Part 1 (SAE of pulmonary edema in Subject (b)(6)) is discussed in the prior section; the other event that was adjudicated as a heart failure hospitalization in Part 1 occurred in a subject who had chronic heart failure at baseline, which was reported as worsening. In Part 2, six subjects experienced events that were adjudicated as a heart failure hospitalization. Of these subjects, one had previously taken aprocitentan 25 mg during Part 1 and five had previously been assigned to placebo. Of those who were switched from placebo, three of the events occurred within the first 3 weeks of initiating aprocitentan. In Part 3, three subjects experienced events that were adjudicated as heart failure hospitalizations; 2 subjects in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm and 1 subject in the placebo arm (Table 32). Narratives for the adjudicated MACE-plus heart failure hospitalizations are provided in Table 144. Heart failure events resulting in discontinuation were uncommon (Table 32).

L501: ## 7.6.5.2. Anemia
L502: Anemia is a known risk of ERAs and is thought to be due in part to hemodilution. In Part 1 of PRECISION, AEs suggestive of anemia (FMQ narrow) were reported in the aprocitentan arms but not in the placebo arm; however, there was no evidence of a dose-response relationship (4% and 1% in the 12.5 mg and 25 mg dose groups, respectively; Table 33). In Part 2, anemia AEs were reported in 9% of aprocitentan-treated. In Part 3, the incidence was similar among treatment groups. All of the AEs were reported to be mild to moderate in severity, with the exception of two AEs. In Part 1, one subject (Subject [REDACTED](b) (6)) experienced severe anemia related to blood loss, and in Part 2, one subject (Subject [REDACTED](b) (6)) experienced severe and serious anemia, which was not associated with a bleeding event and resulted in a blood transfusion. Discontinuation of study treatment due to anemia was reported in one subject (Subject [REDACTED](b) (6)) in
L503: Part 2 who experienced moderate “anemia of unknown etiology” and was treated with a blood transfusion. Reported laboratory values for the two subjects with anemia not associated with a bleeding event show a decrease in hemoglobin of approximately 2-3 g/dL from their baseline. Narratives for these two subjects are provided below. Decreases in hemoglobin observed for the safety population are discussed further below.

L508: ## Anemia Narratives
L509: - Serious AESubject (b)(6) was a 72-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who experienced an SAE of anemia on Study Day 78 to 82. The subject presented to the emergency department with chest pain which was diagnosed as unstable angina pectoris, and a coronary artery bypass graft procedure was scheduled. On the same day, the subject was diagnosed with anemia (reported lab value: Hgb 8.6 g/dL). Fecal occult blood test was positive; however, no active bleeds were noted on endoscopy and colonoscopy. The event resolved after treatment with two units of red blood cells. No action was taken with study treatment and the event was considered not related to study treatment. Hemoglobin at baseline was 11.0 g/dL and reported as 9.1 g/dL and 9.2 g/dL, at the end of Parts 1 and 2, respectively. The last reported measurement was 9.9 g/dL, which was 2 months after the last dose of study drug.
- Resulted in DiscontinuationSubject (b)(6) was a 72-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who received a blood transfusion for anemia of unknown etiology reported as a nonserious AE on Study Day 86-89. During screening on Day -69, it was reported that the subject experienced a nonserious event of anemia of unknown etiology of moderate intensity (reported value: Hgb 11.6 g/dL). During the study, the subject was treated with intramuscular cyanocobalamin from Study Day 42-57 (reported lab values: Hgb 8.8 g/dL, Day 42; Hgb 9.0 g/dL, Day 50; 8.7 g/dL, Day 67) and transfused with one unit of red blood cells on Study Day 86 (Hgb value not provided). Given the need for blood transfusion, the criterion for permanent study drug discontinuation was met and aprocitentan was discontinued on Day 89 (lab values not reported). Four days later on Day 93, hemoglobin was reported as 9.5 g/dL. The last reported hemoglobin was 12.1 g/dL on Day 121 at the safety follow-up visit.
L510: ## Hemoglobin Decrease
L511: Compared to the placebo group, the aprocitentan arms had a larger mean decrease from baseline in hemoglobin at Week 4 of treatment (mean decrease of 0.80 and 0.85 g/dL in aprocitentan 12.5 mg and 25 mg arms, respectively, compared to a decrease of 0.04 g/dL in the placebo arm) (Figure 8). In subjects reassigned from placebo to the aprocitentan 25 mg arm in Part 2, mean decreases from Week 4 baseline values were similar to values observed in subjects initially randomized to 12.5 mg and 25 mg at the start of the study; the mean decreases appeared to stabilize by Week 6 (Figure 8). In Part 3, mean hemoglobin levels rose in subjects rerandomized from aprocitentan to placebo, indicating that the effect on hemoglobin was reversible (Figure 9).

L518: Hemoglobin decreases exceeding specified levels are shown in Table 145. In Part 1, the incidence of a hemoglobin decrease &gt;2 g/dL compared to baseline was greater in subjects receiving aprocitentan (7% of 12.5 mg and 3% of 25 mg arms) compared to subjects treated with placebo (1%). A decrease in hemoglobin from baseline to below 10.0 g/dL in the initial 4-weeks was reported in 3% of subjects taking aprocitentan 12.5 mg, 2% of subjects taking aprocitentan 25 mg, versus 0 subjects taking placebo (Table 145).
L519: ## 7.6.5.3. Hepatotoxicity
L520: ## Adverse Events
L521: ERAs can cause hepatotoxicity (degree of risk varies among members of the class) and, as such, hepatotoxicity was an AESI for aprocitentan. During Part 1, no AEs suggestive of hepatic injury were reported in the aprocitentan arms (Table 34). During Part 2, 6 (0.9%) subjects reported AEs within the Hepatic injury and Hepatic failure narrow FMQ; of these, Hepatitis B was reported for one subject and Hepatitis C was reported for one subject. All of these events were reported to be mild to moderate in severity. In Part 3, AEs with this narrow FMQ were reported in 2.3% of subjects who were rerandomized to placebo arm as compared to &lt;1% of subjects randomized to remain in the aprocitentan arm.

L533: ## Case Level Analysis
L534: To further evaluate the risk of hepatotoxicity, subject level data from the PRECISION study and an additional Phase 2 study (Study 201) were reviewed by DHN. Per the DHN review:
L535: - Based on hepatocellular scatterplots (eDISH), there was no imbalance in liver analytes nor cases concerning for significant liver injury in Study 201.
- Based on hepatocellular scatterplots (eDISH), there was no imbalance in liver analytes during the 4-week double-blinded placebo-controlled period (Part 1) in Study 301.
- DILI adjudication of high interest cases in the PRECISION study identified four cases in Part 2 (aprocitentan 25 mg arm), which included two highly likely cases (based on positive rechallenge), and two possible cases (Table 36).

L546: According to the DHN review, the mean latency period for the highly likely and possible DILI events after initiation of aprocitentan was 134 days (approximately 4 months; range 84-205 days). Of note, DILI with a long latency period has been observed with other ERAs (bosentan, macitentan, ambrisentan). In general, the elevations in liver enzymes resolved/improved with discontinuation of aprocitentan. As previously mentioned, in two cases, elevations reoccurred with aprocitentan rechallenge; these two highly likely cases of DILI are further described below.
L547: - Subject (b) (6): The subject was a 63-year-old white female in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who experienced elevated liver enzymes on Study Day 206; ALT was reported as 284 U/L (8.4 x ULN) and AST was reported as 210 U/L (6.2 x ULN). The subject's baseline medication history included levothyroxine (0.125 mg daily), atorvastatin (20 mg daily). Her atorvastatin dose was increased to 40 mg daily on Study Day 189 (17 days prior to the event). Treatment for the elevated enzymes included stopping atorvastatin on Day 209 and interrupting aprocitentan from Study Days 210 to 222. The event resolved without sequelae on Day 223. On Study Day 312 (61 days after rechallenge), the subject again experienced mild elevation of transaminases, AST was 136 U/L (4 x ULN) and ALT was 84 U/L (2.5 x
L548: ULN) with no symptoms reported; AP rose as well but remained &lt; ULN. Total bilirubin (TB) remained normal throughout. ALT and AST climbed a bit further and the aprocitentan was permanently discontinued on Study Day 316.
L549: At baseline, her enzymes were normal [ALT was 26 U/L, AST was 24 U/L, and TB was 3\mumol/L (SI units)]. She started the study in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg arm, and her ALT and AST remained within normal levels for the 4 weeks, reported as 24 U/L and 20 U/L at the end of Part 1. The subject started aprocitentan 25 mg on Day 28 and her liver enzymes were reported as normal until Study Day 206 (178 days later). She had no associated symptoms. Ultrasound showed steatosis, but no obvious etiology for acute liver injury. No other liver evaluation tests (autoimmune markers, viral tests) were available. Alcohol history was not provided. Figure 10 below provides the liver analytes by Study Day. Return to baseline had not yet occurred at the last follow-up.
L550: Figure 10. Liver Enzyme and Total Bilirubin Levels, by x ULN and Study Day, Subject (b) (6), Safety Population, PRECISION Study

Abbreviations: ALP, alkaline phosphatase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; DHN, Division of Hepatology and Nutrition; DILI, drug-induced liver injury; RI, run-in; SBAT, standard background antihypertensive; SCR, screening; ULN, upper limit of normal; x, times
L552: ## DHN Assessment
L553: This case is assessed as highly likely due to aprocitentan treatment based on the positive rechallenge which strongly suggests a causal relationship with aprocitentan and not the atorvastatin. The statin was not restarted. The latencies from aprocitentan start, dose increase and rechallenge were 208, 179 and 61 days, respectively. The shorter latency on rechallenge would be consistent with DILI. Both rises in aminotransferases declined to normal or near normal with holding aprocitentan. We note that evaluation testing was limited, and so recurrent infection or
L554: passage of gallstones could compete. Her sex and obesity increase her risk of gallstones. However, she was asymptomatic, and ultrasound did not report gallstones. The Applicant suggests the first enzyme peak was due to the statin and the second due to steatosis found on ultrasound. We do not agree. The more logical explanation is a positive aprocitentan rechallenge. Her BMI was 40 \text{ kg/m}^2 at baseline, and there was no mention of marked weight gain followed by weight loss with the second peak. The steatosis is likely chronic and irrelevant to the acute injury.
L555: - Subject (b)(6): The subject was a 36-year-old white female in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who experienced elevated liver enzymes on Study Day 139; ALT, AST, AP, and TB were 145 U/L (4.3 \times \text{ULN}), 75 U/L, 54 U/L and 0.29 mg/dL, respectively. CK was normal. No symptoms were mentioned. The study drug continued without change. On Study Day 147, ALT and AST peaked at 220 U/L (6.5 \times \text{ULN}) and 80 U/L, respectively. Still no symptoms were mentioned. The study drug was held on Study Day 148. The liver enzymes fell to normal or nearly normal by Day 196. On the same day, aprocitentan 25 mg was restarted. On Study Day 240 (44 days after rechallenging), ALT and AST were 78 and 37 U/L, respectively. AP and TB remained &lt; \text{ULN} throughout both aminotransferase elevations. Aprocitentan was permanently discontinued on Day 247 due to the aminotransferase elevations. ALT and AST rose to second peak of 114 (3.3 \times \text{ULN}) and 49 U/L on Study Day 253. Thereafter, ALT improved with &gt;50\% decline from second peak occurring within 27 days, and AST was back to normal (Figure 11). At baseline, the subject's BMI was 28 \text{ kg/m}^2. Relevant medical history included just the targeted disease. Alcohol history was not provided. Concurrent medications relative to DILI risk were nil. The subject's ALT, AST, AP, and TB were 24 U/L, 17 U/L, 50 U/L and 0.2 mg/dL, respectively, at baseline. ALT return to baseline had not yet occurred at last follow-up. Evaluation testing was not done.

Abbreviations: ALP, alkaline phosphatase; ALT, alanine aminotransferase; AST, aspartate aminotransferase; DHN, Division of Hepatology and Nutrition; DILI, drug-induced liver injury; RI, run-in; SBAT, standard background antihypertensive therapy; SCR, screening; ULN, upper limit of normal
L558: ## DHN Assessment
L559: This case was assessed as highly likely DILI due to aprocitentan because the positive rechallenge strongly suggested a causal relationship. The latencies from aprocitentan start and from rechallenge were 140 and 44 days, respectively. The shorter latency on rechallenge would be consistent with DILI. Both rises in aminotransferases declined to normal or near normal with holding aprocitentan. Evaluation testing was limited, and so recurrent infection or passage of gallstones could compete. However, she was asymptomatic. The Applicant noted there was an “absence of apparent confounding factors” and that attribution to aprocitentan could not be “ruled out.” There were no obvious competing etiologies.
L560: ## 7.6.5.4. Hypersensitivity Reactions
L561: During Part 1, hypersensitivity-related AEs were reported in 0.8% of subjects taking aprocitentan 12.5 mg and 0.4% of subjects taking 25 mg compared with none reported for placebo. During Part 2, one subject (Subject (b) (6)) taking 25 mg had an SAE of allergic dermatitis requiring hospitalization. The narrative is provided below.
L562: Subject (b) (6) was a 73-year-old female randomized to placebo during Part 1. On Study Day 31 (2 days after starting aprocitentan 25 mg in Part 2), the subject experienced a nonserious AE of allergic dermatitis on her face, trunk, both arms and legs. Her symptoms intensified and she
L563: was hospitalized with a diagnosis of allergic dermatitis on Day 39 to 43. Study treatment was interrupted on Study Days 40 to 43 and restarted on Day 44. Two days later, on Day 46, the allergic dermatitis (reported as nonserious) recurred (positive rechallenge) and the study medication was permanently discontinued on the same day. The event resolved on Day 50 and was assessed as related to aprocitentan.
L564: ## 7.6.6. MACE
L565: MACE was defined as cardiovascular death, nonfatal myocardial infarction, or non-fatal stroke, and events were adjudicated by the CAC (Table 37). MACE events would be expected in this population and given the design of the study, data are challenging to interpret. On face, they do not raise concern. A discussion of the adjudicated MACE deaths is provided in Section 7.6.2. Narratives for subjects with adjudicated MACE are provided in Table 144.

L573: ## 7.6.7. Pregnancies During the PRECISION Study
L574: One subject (b) (6) discontinued study treatment due to pregnancy during Part 2 of the PRECISION study. Estimated exposure to aprocitentan 25 mg was 7 weeks of the first trimester. A normal baby was born at 38 weeks' gestation. The narrative is provided below:
L575: - Subject (b) (6): A 34-year-old White female informed the study site that she tested positive on a urine pregnancy test on Study Day 139 (b) (6). The study medication (aprocitentan 25 mg) and background therapy were permanently discontinued on the same day. The pregnancy was confirmed by the site (B-hCG was 1157 mIU/mL on Study Day 141). Her previous B-hCG test was negative on Study Day 90 (49 days prior). The Investigator explained to her the potential complications which might affect the fetus/baby. She decided to continue with the pregnancy and was referred to a gynecologist. Three weeks later, the subject visited the ED for increased BP, reported as 180/90 mmHg, and was started on labetalol 100mg twice daily. All obstetrical ultrasounds of the fetus/fetal anatomy were normal, blood tests, genetic tests (Myriad panel and Alpha-Fetoprotein Test) were normal; the estimated delivery date was (b) (6). She completed her Safety Follow-Up visit on Study Day 189 with a normal visit and labs reported. At 38-weeks' gestation, the subject was admitted for labor induction and endorsed good fetal movement. Cesarean section was performed due to failure to progress in labor and a male infant was born with weight 8 lbs 13.1 oz. There were no delivery complications, no neonatal illness, no need for resuscitation, no need for Intensive Care Unit admission, and no congenital anomalies were diagnosed. The subject was discharged from the hospital with routine postpartum follow-up. Baseline characteristics for the subject included obesity (BMI 35.2 kg/m2) and a normal eGFR (119 mL/min/1.73m2). Obstetrical history included five previous pregnancies complicated by preeclampsia; one spontaneous abortion followed by four live births delivered at 39 weeks and all healthy babies. Concomitant medications included ferrous fumarate, escitalopram 10 mg, and use of three methods of contraception (nonoxynol gel as needed, ethinyl estradiol/norethisterone acetate tablets daily, and condom usage as needed).
L576: ## 7.6.8. Laboratory Findings
L577: Laboratory findings associated with AESIs are described in Section 7.6.5. Analyses of other biochemistry data did not reveal findings of concern. A summary of the findings for kidney function is provided below. Overall, a small decrease in eGFR from baseline was observed in subjects taking aprocitentan, with the greatest decrease occurring during the initial 6 weeks of treatment in subjects randomized to aprocitentan 25 mg.

L580: taking the study treatment at the time of laboratory collection (at Week 4 end of Part 1 Visit), due to hospitalization for heart failure, with the last dose of study medication approximately 2 weeks prior. The subject's eGFR at baseline was 38 mL/min/1.73 m2. The narrative for this hospitalization is provided in (MACE-plus) Table 144.

L595: ## - Serious AE
L596: Subject [REDACTED](b) (6) was a 65-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm who had a SAE of gastroenteritis and acute kidney injury due to severe dehydration on Study days 80 to 91. The subject was admitted to the hospital with a diagnosis of acute kidney injury after experiencing diarrhea and vomiting. The subject's creatinine clearance was reported as 2.3 (no unit) upon admission. Study treatment was interrupted on Day 80 and the events resolved with treatment on Day 84. The event was considered not related to the study drug, which was restarted on Day 91. The subject had an eGFR of 34 mL/min/1.73m2 at baseline, and at the start of Part 2 (Day 37), an eGFR of 41 mL/min/1.73m2.

L611: ## 7.6.9.2. Assessment for Potential QT Prolongation
L612: The potential risk for QT prolongation and Torsade de Pointe with aprocitentan was evaluated in a thorough QT study (ID-080-108) which was reviewed by the Interdisciplinary Review Team for Cardiac Safety Studies. The thorough QT study was a prospective, single-center, randomized, double-blind, placebo- and moxifloxacin- controlled, 4-way crossover Phase 1 study to assess the effect of multiple therapeutic and supratherapeutic doses of aprocitentan on the corrected QT interval duration in healthy subjects. The supratherapeutic dose (100 mg), which is 8 times the therapeutic dose (12.5 mg), provided approximately 3-fold high clinical exposure. Data were analyzed using exposure-response analysis as the primary analysis. The results of the study show that at 8 times the maximum recommended dose, clinically significant corrected QT interval prolongation was not observed. Further details can be found in the Interdisciplinary Review Team Review dated August 14, 2023.
L613: ## 7.7. Key Safety Review Issues
L614: ## 7.7.1. Hepatotoxicity
L615: ## Issue
L616: The PRECISION study data suggests aprocitentan can cause elevations of transaminases without hyperbilirubinemia.
L617: ## Background
L618: Hepatotoxicity, including liver failure, has been reported with ERAs and is described in the FDA-approved labeling for these products. Macitentan, the parent compound of aprocitentan, includes hepatotoxicity as a warning and precaution in the labeling and recommends a baseline measurement of liver analytes (including total bilirubin) and repeat measurements as clinically indicated. Two products in the ERA class, bosentan and sparsentan, have a REMS for hepatotoxicity.
L619: ## Assessment
L620: There was no imbalance in findings for liver analytes between aprocitentan and placebo exposure in the PRECISION study or Study 201, although, the randomized periods for comparison were relatively short at four to eight weeks and latencies for other ERA hepatotoxicity have been several months. There were no cases of jaundice in PRECISION and hence no Hy's Law or cholestatic jaundice cases across all study periods. The characterization of the risk is limited due to the size of the study population.
L621: Case level analysis of the PRECISION study safety data by DHN suggests that aprocitentan can cause hepatotoxicity. In Part 2, there were two subjects with liver enzyme elevations of greater than 5-fold the upper limit of normal which were assessed as highly likely related to the use of aprocitentan. Both subjects had positive rechallenges. One subject had potential hepatocellular injury (i.e., predominantly aminotransferase elevation) while the other had a mixed injury (i.e.,
L622: both aminotransferase and alkaline phosphatase elevation). The enzyme elevations were modest without hyperbilirubinemia and were resolved or nearly resolved at the last follow-up visit after aprocitentan discontinuation.
L623: ## Conclusion
L624: Although data indicate potential risk, the findings in the development program as well as the findings with macitentan suggest that risk can be adequately mitigated with labeling, which will include a warning and precaution for hepatotoxicity. Labeling will include a recommendation for monitoring of liver analytes, to be checked prior to initiation and periodically during treatment. Aprocitentan will not be recommended for use in patients with decompensated cirrhosis or acute liver disease. Postmarketing surveillance will include enhanced pharmacovigilance for a period of 5 years with required submission of all serious cases of liver injury as 15-day alert reports and summary analyses provided in the periodic safety reports.
L625: ## 7.7.2. Embryo-Fetal Toxicity
L626: ## Issue
L627: Nonclinical data with macitentan suggest an adverse effect of aprocitentan on the developing fetus when administered to a pregnant woman. In EFD studies with aprocitentan, while no embryo-fetal toxicity, including teratogenic effects, were observed at 5-fold and 29-fold the human exposure at MRHD in rats and rabbits, respectively, the highest dose of aprocitentan tested in the rat EFD study was too low to fully characterize reproductive risk. There are uncertainties about the embryo-fetal toxicity risk of ERAs in humans. Data regarding pregnancy outcomes in first trimester exposure of ERAs are limited.
L628: ## Background
L629: Female adults of reproductive potential (FRP; ages 18-49 years) are included in the target population for aprocitentan and represented 5% of the PRECISION study population. The estimated background risk of major birth defects and miscarriage for the indicated population is unknown. In the general U.S. population, the estimated background risk of major birth defects is 2-4% and risk of miscarriage is 15-20%. Hypertension during pregnancy is considered to be high-risk. Prescribers of antihypertensives commonly use inhibitors of the renin-angiotensin system, particularly angiotensin-converting enzyme inhibitors and ARBs, in the treatment of hypertension in FRP. These products have a boxed warning for fetal toxicity when used in the second and third trimester and should be discontinued as soon as possible when pregnancy is detected. Valsartan (ARB) was used as background therapy in the PRECISION study.
L630: ## Assessment
L631: The PRECISION study excluded pregnant subjects and required monthly pregnancy tests and contraception use. There was one unplanned pregnancy reported during Part 2 of PRECISION (Study Day 139). The subject had taken aprocitentan 25 mg daily for approximately 6 weeks (first trimester exposure) prior to discontinuation due to the pregnancy. The outcome was reported as a normal baby born at 38 weeks gestation (see Section 7.6.6 for details).
L632: Aprocitentan is not proposed as first-line therapy which may limit its use and minimize the risk in FRP. In addition, all ERA products are approved with a REMS with elements to assure safe use (ETASU) to mitigate the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity. The Division of Cardiology and Nephrology and the Division of Risk Management agree that a REMS with ETASU is also necessary for aprocitentan. However, the REMS for aprocitentan will differ from the REMS for other ERA products (approved for PAH or IgA nephropathy). The elements agreed upon by these FDA Divisions for the REMS for aprocitentan consist of ETASU A and B, an implementation system, and a timetable for submission of assessments. Prescriber certification and specialized training (ETASU A) on the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity helps ensure all prescribers are educated on the risk. Specialized training is expected to improve identification of at-risk patients and encourage healthcare providers to counsel patients about the risk and the actions to take to prevent fetal exposure and minimize exposure during pregnancy. Pharmacy certification (ETASU B) helps ensure only certified pharmacies are dispensing aprocitentan after verifying that prescribers of aprocitentan are certified. The REMS for aprocitentan is structured to ease healthcare burden while supporting the safe use of aprocitentan. For a detailed description, see the REMS review by Cristen Lambert, PharmD.
L633: In addition to the REMS, the risk will be included in labeling as a Boxed Warning and approval will include a postmarketing requirement for a descriptive study of pregnancy outcomes.
L634: ## Conclusion
L635: The risk of embryo-fetal toxicity with aprocitentan can be mitigated with the use of effective contraception in FRP. A REMS with ETASU is required to ensure the benefits outweigh the risk; the ETASU is necessary to ensure prescribers are aware of the risk and the need to counsel patients on the use of contraception and regular pregnancy testing to prevent and minimize fetal exposure to aprocitentan. Pharmacy certification is necessary to confirm that the prescriber is certified in the REMS prior to dispensing of aprocitentan to patients. Further, the risk will be communicated in labeling and an observational pregnancy safety study will be required to assist in further characterizing the risk.
L636: ## 8. Therapeutic Individualization
L637: ## 8.1. Intrinsic Factors
L638: ## Renal Impairment
L639: A 50 mg dose of aprocitentan was studied in subjects with severe renal impairment and compared to age-, sex-, body weight-, and height-matched subjects with normal renal function in Study AC-080-105. The maximum plasma concentration (C_{max}) was similar between groups, with a geometric mean ratio of 1.04 (90% CI 0.85-1.28). The time to maximum concentration (T_{max}) increased from 5 hours in healthy controls to 7.6 hours in subjects with severe renal impairment. A lower clearance led to a longer half-life in those with severe renal impairment, increasing from 47.4 hours in healthy subjects to 53.2 hours in those with severe renal impairment. The AUC_{0-t} and AUC_{0-\infty} were 32% and 34% higher, respectively, in those with severe renal impairment compared to healthy subjects. Please see Section 14.2 for a full
L640: discussion of results. No dose adjustment is required in patients with mild to severe renal impairment. Because the effect of kidney failure or dialysis on aprocitentan pharmacokinetics has not been studied, use of aprocitentan in these patients is not recommended.
L641: ## Hepatic Impairment
L642: A 25 mg dose of aprocitentan was studied in subjects with moderate hepatic impairment and compared to age-, sex-, body weight-, and height-matched subjects with normal hepatic function in Study ID-080-109. The C_{max} was similar between groups, with a geometric mean ratio of 1.03 (90% CI 0.86-1.24). The T_{max} was 4 hours for healthy controls and those with moderate hepatic impairment. The mean half-life increased from 48.3 hours in healthy subjects to 56.4 hours in those with moderate hepatic impairment. The AUC_{0-t} and AUC_{0-\infty} were 22% and 23% higher, respectively, in those with moderate hepatic impairment compared to healthy subjects. Please see Section 14.2 for a full discussion of results. Based on the results, no dose adjustment is required in patients with mild or moderate hepatic impairment. Because the effect of severe hepatic impairment on aprocitentan pharmacokinetics has not been studied, use of aprocitentan in these patients is not recommended.
L643: ## Age
L644: Elderly subjects (inclusion criteria: 65-80 years, inclusive) generally had higher C_{max} and AUC_{t} values in Study AC-080-101. After multiple doses, the C_{max} and AUC_{t} ratio of geometric means were 1.31 and 1.21 for elderly compared to nonelderly subjects (inclusion criteria: 18 to 45 years, inclusive), respectively. Please see Section 14.2 for a full discussion of results. No dose adjustment is recommended for elderly subjects taking aprocitentan.
L645: ## Race
L646: A 25 mg dose of aprocitentan was studied in healthy Japanese and Caucasian subjects in Study ID-080-107. Overall, after multiple doses, the C_{max} and AUC_{t} ratio of geometric means were 1.02 and 1.08 for Japanese subjects compared to Caucasian subjects, respectively. Please see Section 14.2 for a full discussion of results. No dose adjustment is recommended based on race for aprocitentan.
L647: ## Sex
L648: Females generally had higher plasma concentrations compared to males in Study AC-080-101. After a single dose, the C_{max} and AUC_{0-\infty} ratio of geometric means were 1.25 and 1.27 for females compared to males, respectively. After multiple doses, the C_{max} and AUC_{t} ratio of geometric means were 1.38 and 1.37, respectively, for females compared to males. Please see Section 14.2 for a full discussion of results. No dose adjustment is recommended for females taking aprocitentan.
L649: ## 8.2. Extrinsic Factors
L650: ## Drug Interactions
L651: ## Metabolic Pathway
L652: Based on the mass balance study (Study AC-080-104), two metabolic pathways contribute to \ge 25\% of aprocitentan elimination – glucosidation of aprocitentan to metabolite M3 by UGT1A1 and UGT2B7 (accounts for approximately 25% of the administered dose), and hydrolysis of aprocitentan to metabolite M1 which then undergoes several other reactions leading to metabolites A1, A4, A5, M4, M5, M6, M11, and M13 (accounts for approximately 31.5% of the administered dose).
L653: While M1 accounts for 31.5% of the administered dose, based on results from in vitro studies, it is believed that M1 is formed by both chemical and enzymatic hydrolysis. Incubation of radiolabeled aprocitentan with human liver microsomes led formation of M1 (4.3% of total chromatogram radioactivity); however, M1 was also observed in experiments without microsomes or a nicotinamide adenine dinucleotide phosphate-regenerating system (present at 9.3% and 6.4% total chromatogram radioactivity, respectively, in comparable conditions). When incubated with hepatocytes, M1-M6 was generated, but no metabolite contributed to more than 5% of total chromatogram radioactivity. M1 was also seen in the control experiment without liver cells and accounted for 8.7% of total chromatogram radioactivity.
L654: For a complete discussion of aprocitentan metabolism, please see in vitro studies in Section 14.1 and the mass-balance study in Section 14.2.
L655: ## Effect of Other Drugs on Aprocitentan
L656: As glucosidation of aprocitentan to metabolite M3 accounts for approximately 25.2% of the administered dose, uridine diphosphate-glucuronosyltransferase (UGT) inducers may lead to a decrease in aprocitentan concentration and efficacy; however, no clinical drug-drug interaction studies were conducted with aprocitentan and UGT inducers.
L657: ## Effect of Aprocitentan on Other Drugs
L658: The effect of 25 mg aprocitentan at steady state was evaluated on a single dose of 10 mg rosuvastatin, a breast cancer resistance protein substrate, in healthy male subjects. Aprocitentan increased the C_{\max} of rosuvastatin by 40%. The AUC_{0-t} and AUC_{0-\infty} were minimally affected (geometric mean ratios of 1.07 and 0.99, respectively). The mean half-life of rosuvastatin decreased from 17.9 hours to 14.1 hours, though this change was not statistically significant (p=0.14).
L659: The effect of a 50 mg aprocitentan dose at steady state concentrations was evaluated on a single dose of 8 mg midazolam, a cytochrome P450 3A4 substrate, in healthy male subjects. Aprocitentan did not have an effect on the C_{\max} of midazolam (ratio of geometric means 1.04, 90% CI 0.88-1.23); however, the C_{\max} of 1-hydroxy midazolam was decreased (ratio of geometric means 0.86, 90% CI 0.7-1.1). The mean half-life for midazolam was unchanged when administered with aprocitentan; however, the mean half-life of 1-hydroxy midazolam increased
L660: to a mean of 7.7 hours when administered with aprocitentan compared to 5.8 hours when midazolam was administered alone. The AUC_{0-t} and AUC_{0-\infty} of midazolam were minimally increased when administered with aprocitentan (both ratios of geometric means were 1.14). The AUC_{0-t} and AUC_{0-\infty} of 1-hydroxy midazolam were minimally decreased when administered with aprocitentan (geometric mean ratios of 0.95 and 0.98, respectively).
L661: ## Dosing Recommendations
L662: No dose adjustments related to drug interactions are proposed. UGT inducers may decrease aprocitentan concentrations and thus may lower the efficacy of aprocitentan, though the extent to which aprocitentan concentration and efficacy may decrease has not been studied. UGT inducers may not produce any clinically meaningful effect on aprocitentan concentration, as only 25.2% of the dose is metabolized though glucosidation, and multiple UGT enzymes are involved in aprocitentan glucosidation. If aprocitentan is an ineffective treatment in patients on UGT inducers, these patients will likely be treated as other nonresponders, and discontinuation is likely. Therefore, labeling language for those on UGT inducers is not necessary.
L663: ## Food Effect
L664: In Study AC-080-101, effects of food on plasma PK variables were assessed after a single 100 mg dose of aprocitentan. Exposure increased with food, with the geometric mean ratios (fed/fasted) of 1.73 and 1.23 for C_{max} and AUC_{t-\infty}, respectively. The T_{max} decreased from 9 to 5 hours when fed, though the range was similar (4 to 10 hours for fasted, and 5 to 12 hours for fed). The labeling states that aprocitentan can be taken with or without food. The labeling is in line with the Phase 3 study where subjects were to take aprocitentan each morning, irrespective of food intake.
L665: ## 8.3. Plans for Pediatric Drug Development
L666: The investigational product triggers the Pediatric Research Equity Act as a new active ingredient for the proposed indication. The Applicant submitted their agreed upon Agreed Initial Pediatric Study Plan requesting a full waiver of pediatric assessments because “a favorable therapeutic benefit/risk profile is not expected,” other “safe and efficacious therapeutic classes of antihypertensive drugs can be used,” aprocitentan does not represent a meaningful therapeutic benefit over existing therapies, and because of the potential for teratogenicity.
L667: Based on the available data, the Division and Pediatric Review Committee agree that a full waiver of pediatric studies should be granted because the product fails to represent a meaningful therapeutic benefit over existing therapies for pediatric patients and is unlikely to be used in a substantial number of pediatric patients with hypertension. Given the product’s safety profile, aprocitentan will not be approved as initial (first-line) therapy for the treatment of hypertension in adults. While the development program demonstrated that the product is effective in lowering blood pressure in adults, the size of the effect was modest (LS mean difference between aprocitentan and placebo of ~4 mmHg). Given these issues, the benefit-risk profile is expected to be unfavorable for pediatric patients with hypertension.
L668: ## 8.4. Pregnancy, Lactation, and Females/Males of Reproductive Potential
L669: Embryo-fetal toxicities in rats and rabbits, the results of male and female fertility studies in rats, and potential reproductive organ toxicities are discussed in Section 7.1. Nonclinical data supporting labeling language are shown in the table below by labeling section.
L670: **Table 40. Nonclinical Data Supporting Labeling on Fertility, Pregnancy and Lactation**
L671: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
8.1 Pregnancy	
Animal data
In pregnant rats, oral administration of macitentan at doses of 3, 10 and 150 mg/kg/day throughout organogenesis, resulted in fetal abnormalities, which included defects in the cardiovascular and mandibular arch fusion malformations, at all the doses tested. The apocitentan exposure at the lowest dose tested (3 mg/kg/day) was approximately equivalent to the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
In pregnant rabbits, oral administration of macitentan at doses of 2.5, 12.5 and 25 mg/kg/day throughout organogenesis, resulted in fetal abnormalities, which included defects in the cardiovascular and mandibular arch fusion malformation, at all the doses tested. The apocitentan exposure at the lowest dose tested (2.5 mg/kg/day) was approximately 15-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
In the pre- and postnatal developmental (PPND) study in rats, oral administration of macitentan at doses of 10, 50 or 250 mg/kg/day during pregnancy and the lactational period resulted in reduced pup survival and impairment of the male fertility of the offspring at all doses. The apocitentan exposure at the lowest dose tested (10 mg/kg/day) was approximately 2-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.

8.2 Lactation	
In rats, apocitentan, which is an active metabolite of macitentan, was consistently observed in milk samples across all time points after oral administration of 14C-labeled macitentan.

8.3 Females and Males of Reproductive Potential	
Other ERAs have shown an adverse effect on spermatogenesis in humans and/or animals. Apocitentan may impair fertility in males of reproductive potential. It is not known whether the effects on fertility would be reversible.

13.1 Impairment of Fertility	
In a fertility and early embryonic development (FEED) study in female rats, oral administration of apocitentan prior to mating at doses of 10, 50, or 250 mg/kg/day resulted in minimally-increased pre-implantation loss at \ge50 mg/kg/day, which is approximately 23-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC at 250 mg/kg/day. No impact on fertility was observed at 10 mg/kg/day, which represents 5-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.
In male rats, oral administration of apocitentan for 15 weeks had no effect on fertility or spermatogenesis at doses up to 250 mg/kg, which is approximately 52-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC. In repeat dose toxicity studies, oral administration of apocitentan resulted in testicular tubular degeneration/atrophy in male rats and dogs at the high dose of 250 mg/kg/day and at doses \ge25 mg/kg/day, respectively, which is approximately 41- and 53-fold the AUC at the MRHD, respectively. The testicular toxicity was not evident in male rats and dogs at 50 mg/kg/day and 5 mg/kg/day, respectively, which
L672: Labeling Section	Nonclinical Data
represents approximately 14- and 11-fold the clinical exposure at the MRHD based on AUC.

L674: Abbreviations: AUC, area under the curve; ^{14}\text{C}, carbon 14 isotope radiolabel; ERA, endothelin receptor antagonist; FEED, fertility and early embryonic development; MRHD, maximum recommended high dose, PPND, postnatal developmental
L675: ## 9. Product Quality
L676: The Office of Pharmaceutical Quality review team has assessed NDA 217686 with respect to chemistry, manufacturing, and controls and has determined that it meets all applicable standards to support the identity, strength, quality, and purity that it purports. As such, the Office of Pharmaceutical Quality recommends approval of this NDA from a quality perspective.
L677: ## 9.1. Device or Combination Product Considerations
L678: Not applicable.
L679: ## 10. Human Subjects Protections/Clinical Site and Other Good Clinical Practice Inspections/Financial Disclosure Review
L680: The Applicant has adequately disclosed financial arrangements with clinical investigators and the PRECISION study appears to have been conducted in compliance with U.S. regulations pertaining to Good Clinical Practice. No clinical sites were inspected. The primary efficacy findings were assessed after a randomized, double-blind treatment period and were based on objective and standardized measurements. None of the PRECISION study sites enrolled a large number of study subjects or drove the primary efficacy findings and review of the data did not raise other serious concerns that would warrant a clinical site inspection (e.g., suspicion of fraud, scientific misconduct, significant human subject protection violations or adverse event profiles).
L681: ## 11. Advisory Committee Summary
L682: The application provides substantial evidence of the product's effectiveness in lowering blood pressure. Given the safety profile of aprocitentan, the product will not be approved as initial (first-line) therapy for the treatment of hypertension in adults. Given the intended use as well as the measures that could be put in place to mitigate risk, the application was not felt to raise significant or controversial issues that would merit outside expertise or public discussion. As such, no Advisory Committee Meeting was held for this application.
L683: ## III. Additional Analyses and Information
L684: ## 12. Summary of Regulatory History
L685: ACT-132577, later called aprocitentan, was developed by Actelion Pharmaceuticals, Ltd. (Actelion) for the treatment of resistant hypertension under IND 122772. The IND was opened on October 17, 2014, with a single-center, three-part, phase 1 study to investigate the safety, tolerability, pharmacokinetics, and pharmacodynamics of ACT-132577 in healthy male and female subjects. The study was deemed safe to proceed on November 20, 2014.
L686: On June 19, 2017, sponsorship of the IND was transferred from Actelion to Idorsia Pharmaceuticals Ltd. (Idorsia) who continued developing aprocitentan for the treatment of hypertension, to lower blood pressure, in adult subjects who are not adequately controlled on three antihypertensive drugs from different classes, including a diuretic. Idorsia did not develop aprocitentan as a first-line monotherapy but rather as a background of other antihypertensives.
L687: Aprocitentan's clinical development program mainly consisted of 10 clinical pharmacology studies and 2 studies in subjects with hypertension:
L688: - Study AC-080A201: A phase 2, double-blind, placebo-controlled, dose-finding study investigating the blood pressure reduction effect of aprocitentan as monotherapy in naïve or antihypertensive subjects
- Study 080A301: A multi-center, blinded, randomized, parallel-group, phase 3 study with aprocitentan in subjects with resistant hypertension (PRECISION)
L689: The Division of Cardiology and Nephrology (DCN) provided feedback on several aspects of product development via advice letters dated August 17, 2015, January 12, 2018, November 23, 2018, April 29, 2019, July 17, 2019, November 13, 2019, June 15, 2020, November 4, 2020, and December 02, 2021. Additional information on milestone meetings and key events may be found in the below table.
L690: **Table 41. Summary of Key Regulatory History, PRECISION Study**
L691: Topic	Key Regulatory History
Milestone meetings and key events	
Jul 28, 2014: Pre-IND meeting
Oct 17, 2014: IND-opening study submitted
Jun 19, 2017: Change of ownership from Actelion Pharmaceuticals Ltd. to Idorsia Pharmaceuticals Ltd.
Sep 15, 2017: End-of-phase 2 meeting
Sep 22, 2022: PRECISION Study Topline results meeting
Mar 3, 2022: Pre-NDA meeting planned (cancelled by Idorsia after receiving DCN's meeting preliminary comments)
Jun 2, 2022: Type C REMS meeting
Nov 28, 2022: Pre-NDA meeting planned (cancelled by Idorsia after receiving DCN's meeting preliminary comments)
Dec 19, 2022: Idorsia submitted an NDA under section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act
L692: Source: DARRTS
L693: Abbreviations: DCN, FDA Division of Cardiology and Nephrology; IND, investigational new drug, Ltd., limited; NDA, new drug application; DARRTS: FDA's Document Archiving, Reporting and Regulatory Tracking System
L694: On December 19, 2022, pursuant to Section 505(b)(1) of the Federal Food, Drug, and Cosmetic Act, Idorsia submitted a marketing application for aprocitentan relying on the results of PRECISION. The review classification was Standard, and the user fee goal date was December 19, 2023.
L695: Along with the original NDA submission, Idorsia submitted the proposed proprietary name TRYVIO. This name was considered conditionally acceptable by the Division of Medication Error Prevention and Analysis (DMEPA). A Grant Letter was issued on March 16, 2023.
L696: After consultation with the REMS Oversight Committee (ROC), the Division of Risk Management (DRM) and DCN concluded that the [REDACTED](b) (4) REMS proposed by Idorsia was not adequate to mitigate the risk of embryo-fetal toxicity associated with aprocitentan. It was determined that a REMS with Elements to Assure Safe Use (ETASU) was necessary to mitigate this risk. On August 8, 2023, the Agency shared this decision with Idorsia asking them to submit a revised REMS to the NDA file. On September 6, 2023, Idorsia submitted the revised REMS. The September 6, 2023, submission constituted a major amendment to the application. The user fee goal date was extended by three months; making the new goal date March 19, 2024.
L697: Of note, in the agreed iPSP dated March 16, 2018, Idorsia requested a full waiver of the requirement to submit pediatric assessments for aprocitentan because a favorable therapeutic benefit/risk profile is not expected in the pediatric population. Specifically, given that aprocitentan was developed as a 4th-line add-on therapy in patients with difficult-to-control hypertension and due to the safety concerns of Endothelin Receptor Antagonists (ERAs), the use of aprocitentan was not justified in any age group of the pediatric population with hypertension. The application was discussed with the Pediatric Review Committee (PeRC) on January 9, 2024. The PeRC agreed with granting a full waiver on the basis that TRYVIO fails to represent a
L698: meaningful therapeutic benefit over existing therapies and is unlikely to be used in the pediatric population.
L699: ## 13. Pharmacology Toxicology
L700: ## 13.1. Summary Review of Studies Submitted With the Investigational New Drug Application
L701: ## 13.1.1. Pharmacology
L702: ## 13.1.1.1. Primary Pharmacology
L703: Evaluation of pharmacological activity of aprocitentan on endothelin (ET) receptors are summarized in the table below. In in vitro studies using Chinese hamster ovary (CHO) cells expressing human ET_A or ET_B receptors, aprocitentan showed antagonistic properties at both ET receptor subtypes (ET_A and ET_B) in a competitive radioligand binding assay and inhibited the ET-1-induced increase in cytosolic Ca^{2+} in a functional assay. In the isolated rat aorta and trachea, aprocitentan inhibited ET-1 (ET_A-mediated) and sarafotoxin S6c (ET_B-mediated) induced contractions, respectively. In the in vivo study, aprocitentan dose-dependently increased ET-1 concentrations at doses \ge 3 mg/kg in normotensive rats, suggesting that aprocitentan blocked the binding of ET-1 to ET_B receptors in normal rats.
L704: **Table 42. Aprocitentan Receptor Subtype Selectivity and Functions**
L705: Study	Findings
Antagonist potency in CHO cells expressing human ET_A and ET_B receptors.	Aprocitentan inhibited binding of ^{125}I-ET-1 to ET_A and ET_B receptors with IC_{50} values of 3.4\pm 0.4 nM (n=4) and 987\pm 185 nM (n=4), respectively.
Inhibition of intracellular calcium in CHO-K1 cells expressing recombinant human ET_A or ET_B receptors.	Aprocitentan inhibited the ET-1-induced increase in cytosolic Ca^{2+} with a geometric mean K_b value of 5.5 nM on ET_A (n=5) and 319 nM on ET_B (n=4).
Inhibition of ET-1-induced contraction in isolated rat aorta (denuded endothelium) and trachea (denuded epithelium).	ET_A-mediated contraction: aprocitentan inhibited the ET-1-induced contraction in rat aorta (pA_2 value 6.7\pm 0.2)
ET_B-mediated contraction: aprocitentan inhibited the sarafotoxin S6c-mediated contraction in rat trachea (pA_2 value 5.5\pm 0.3)
Effect of aprocitentan (0.3, 1, 3, 10, 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg, oral gavage, single) on ET-1 plasma concentrations in normotensive male Wistar rats.	Aprocitentan dose-dependently increased ET-1 concentrations at doses \ge 3 mg/kg (starting at plasma concentration of 1.53\pm 0.04 pg/mL) compared to vehicle in normotensive Wistar rats, suggesting that aprocitentan blocked the binding of ET-1 to ET_B receptors in normal rats.

L707: ## NDA 217686
## TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
L708: Proof-of-concept for the efficacy of aprocitentan was assessed in in vivo animal models. As summarized in the table below, various hypertension rat models were used to evaluate potential effects of aprocitentan on hemodynamic parameters.
L709: Aprocitentan showed acute antihypertensive effects, including decreased mean arterial pressure (MAP), in salt-sensitive and volume-dependent hypertensive (i.e., deoxycorticosterone acetate [DOCA]-salt rat and Dahl-S rat) or spontaneously hypertensive (SHR) rat models. The long-term effect of aprocitentan on hemodynamics and potential for end organ damage was further assessed in DOCA-salt rats. Results showed that aprocitentan improved renal blood flow and renal vascular resistance, in addition to decreased MAP, and a tendency for improved left ventricular weight, aorta and coronary artery thickness, and N-terminal pro-brain natriuretic peptide (NT-proBNP) plasma concentration.
L710: **Table 43. Pharmacodynamic Efficacy of Aprocitentan in Animal Models of Hypertension**
L711: Study	Findings
Study No.: B-14.016
Study title: Effect of ACT-132577 on blood pressure and heart rate in conscious hypertensive rats.
Species/strain: DOCA-salt rat (established with combination of unilateral nephrectomy, implantation of pellets of the mineralocorticoid analog DOCA and supply of 1% sodium chloride in drinking water), Dahl-S rat (genetic salt-sensitive model with a supply of 1% sodium chloride in drinking water), and SHR.
Number/sex/group: 6/male/group
Dose: DOCA-salt and Dahl-S rats: 0, 0.3, 1, 3, 10, 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg
SHR: 0, 1, 3, 10, 30, 100, and 300 mg/kg
Route and dosing frequency: Oral gavage/single.
Method: Hemodynamic parameters were measured via radiotelemetry implanted devices.	DOCA-salt rat
Dose-dependent decrease mean arterial pressure (MAP) in conscious rats without affecting heart rate (HR). Maximum decrease in blood pressure (BP) was 41\pm 7 mmHg at 300 mg/kg and the ED_{50}\ge 3 mg/kg.
Dahl-S rat
Dose-dependent decrease MAP in conscious rats without affecting HR. Maximum decrease in BP was 35\pm 6 mmHg at 100 mg/kg and the ED_{50}\ge 3 mg/kg.
SHR
Dose-dependent decrease MAP in conscious rats without affecting HR. Maximum decrease in BP was 18\pm 4 mmHg at 100 mg/kg and the ED_{50}\ge 3 mg/kg.
Study No.: B-16.033
Study title: Chronic Effects of ACT-132577 in DOCA-salt Hypertensive Rats
Species/strain: Rat/Wistar (DOCA-salt).
Number/sex/group: 6 (Set I) and 8-10 (Set II)/male/group.
Dose: Aprocitentan 1, 10 and 100 mg/kg/day
Route and dosing frequency: Oral gavage/ daily for 4 weeks.
Method: Hemodynamic parameters were measured via radiotelemetry implanted devices.	Aprocitentan dose-dependently decreased MAP (2, 19 and 21 mmHg decrease at 1, 10 and 100 mg/kg/day, respectively) and improved renal blood flow and renal vascular resistance.
Aprocitentan tended to improve body weight gain, left ventricular weight, aorta, and heart coronary artery thickness, as well as NT-proBNP plasma concentrations (control: 31.4\pm 3 pg/mL, DOCA-vehicle: 336\pm 128 pg/mL, ACT-132577 1 mg: 282\pm 72 pg/mL, 10 mg: 202\pm 31 pg/mL, 100 mg: 193\pm 32 pg/mL). Aprocitentan did not ameliorate relative right kidney weight, kidney histopathological changes (hydronephrosis, tubulonephrosis, interstitial nephritis and glomerular changes), heart weights and cardiomyopathy (interstitial fibrosis with inflammatory cell infiltration and a minimal to moderate coronary thickening (tunica media and intima)) when compared to control DOCA-salt treatment rats. No effect on water intake and urine volume was observed.

L716: Study	Findings
Study no.: B-15.067
Study title: Effect of aprocitentan or Spironolactone or in Combination with Valsartan on Blood Pressure and Heart Rate in Conscious DOCA-Salt Rats
Species/strain: Rat/Wistar (DOCA salt)
Number/sex/group: 6-10/male/group
Dose: aprocitentan 10 mg/kg, spironolactone 300 mg/kg, and valsartan 30 mg/kg
Route and dosing frequency: Oral gavage/single	Aprocitentan (\downarrow 29\pm 6 mmHg at 10 mg/kg) valsartan (\downarrow 12\pm 5 mmHg at 30 mg/kg), and spironolactone (\downarrow 20\pm 5 mmHg at 300 mg/kg) decreased BP in DOCA-salt rats. Coadministration of valsartan (30 mg/kg) with aprocitentan (10 mg/kg) decreased the MAP beyond the predicted additive value (\downarrow 5\pm 5, \downarrow 22\pm 6, and \downarrow 29\pm 4 mmHg in valsartan, aprocitentan and combination of both, respectively), demonstrating a synergism between the two compounds. Coadministration of valsartan (30 mg/kg) with spironolactone (300 mg/kg) decreased the MAP to the predicted additive value (\downarrow 4\pm 2, \downarrow 14\pm 4 and \downarrow 18\pm 5 mmHg in valsartan, aprocitentan and combination of both, respectively) of both compounds, demonstrating an additive effect between the two compounds. Aprocitentan and spironolactone, alone or in combination, decreased MAP without affecting HR.
Study no.: B-18.042
Study title: Effect of aprocitentan or Spironolactone alone or in Combination With Enalapril on Blood Pressure And Heart Rate In Conscious DOCA-salt Rats
Species/Strain: Rat/Wistar (DOCA-salt)
Number/sex/group: 9-10/male/group
Dose: aprocitentan 10 mg/kg, spironolactone 300 mg/kg, and enalapril 10 mg/kg
Route and dosing frequency: Oral gavage/single	Aprocitentan (\downarrow 29\pm 6 mmHg at \ge 100 mg/kg), enalapril (\downarrow 13\pm 3 mmHg at \ge 100 mg/kg) and spironolactone (\downarrow 20\pm 5 mmHg at \ge 100 mg/kg) decreased BP in DOCA-salt rats. Coadministration of enalapril with aprocitentan decreased the MAP to the predicted additive value (\downarrow 5\pm 2, \downarrow 22\pm 3, and \downarrow 27\pm 4 mmHg in enalapril, aprocitentan and combination of both, respectively) of both compounds. Coadministration of enalapril with spironolactone decreased the MAP to the predicted additive value (\downarrow 4\pm 2, \downarrow 14\pm 4, and \downarrow 17\pm 4 mmHg in enalapril, spironolactone and combination of both, respectively) of both compounds as well. No change in HR was observed in all treatment groups.

Species/Strain: Rat/Wistar (DOCA-salt)

Coadministration of amlodipine with aprocitentan decreased the MAP to the predicted additive value (\downarrow 14\pm 5, \downarrow 18\pm 4, and \downarrow 26\pm 5 mmHg in amlodipine, aprocitentan and combination of both, respectively) of the two compounds. Coadministration of amlodipine with spironolactone decreased the MAP to the predicted additive value (\downarrow 8\pm 3, \downarrow 14\pm 4, and \downarrow 16\pm 4 mmHg in amlodipine, spironolactone, and combination of both, respectively) of the combination of both compounds as well. No significant change in HR was observed in combination experiments.

Study no.: B-14.043
Study title: Effect of aprocitentan or Spironolactone Alone or in Combination with Enalapril on Blood Pressure and Heart Rate in Conscious Spontaneously Hypertensive Rats

Number/sex/group: 3-10/male/group
