L1789: **Table 140. Subject Narratives for Treatment-Emergent Deaths Adjudicated as MACE, Safety Population, PRECISION Study**
L1790: Subject ID	Narrative
(b) (6)	
The 59-year-old White male experienced sudden death on Study Day 57. The subject began the study in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg arm and completed Part 1, switching to aprocitentan 25 mg for Part 2 on Study Day 33. On the morning of Day 57, the subject fainted, wheezed, and died. His wife called the ambulance, and emergency responders confirmed his death upon arrival. No diagnostic or treatment procedures were performed. Prior to the event, the subject's condition was reported as good, with no symptoms of fluid retention, and the subject's blood pressure was reported as 112/66 mmHg at the last visit on Day 46. Relevant medical conditions were reported as hypertension (9 years), previous cerebrovascular accident (7 years prior), myocardial ischemia, angina pectoris, chronic cardiac failure, supraventricular extrasystoles, type 2 diabetes mellitus, dyslipidemia, carotid arteriosclerosis, cerebral ischemia, and chronic pancreatitis. At baseline, he had a BMI of 30.5 kg/m2 and an eGFR of 60 mL/min/1.73 m2. Concomitant medications at baseline included: acetylsalicylic acid (100 mg daily), metformin (1000 mg twice daily), insulin human (30 IU twice daily), and atorvastatin (40 mg daily).

(b) (6)	
The 68-year-old White male experienced death on Study Day 223. The subject began the study in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg arm and completed Part 1, switching to aprocitentan 25 mg for Part 2 on Study Day 29. On Day 253, the subject did not report to the scheduled visit and the site called to follow up. The wife stated the subject died during his sleep on Day 223. No autopsy was conducted. Prior to the event, the subject's blood pressure was reported as 145/62 mmHg at the last visit on Day 190. Relevant medical conditions were reported as hypertension (35 years), diabetes mellitus, dyslipidemia, hepatic steatosis, and sleep apnea syndrome. At baseline, he had a BMI of 35.7 kg/m2 and an eGFR of 87 mL/min/1.73 m2. Concomitant medications at baseline included: acetylsalicylic acid (100 mg daily), metformin (850 mg three times daily), insulin human (dose unspecified), dapagliflozin (10 mg daily), dulaglutide (1.5 mg sq weekly), fenofibrate (200 mg daily) and atorvastatin (40 mg daily).

(b) (6)	
The 64-year-old White female experienced infiltrative carcinoma in the left breast on Study Day 129 which led to the subject's death on Study Day 213. The subject began the study in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg arm and completed Part 1, switching to aprocitentan 25 mg for Part 2 on Study Day 31. The subject was diagnosed with breast cancer and began chemotherapy until Study Day 213 when she was hospitalized for an unspecified reason and died the same day. The primary cause of death was reported as invasive breast carcinoma. Autopsy revealed cerebral edema and subdural hemorrhage at the time of death. Relevant ongoing medical conditions at Screening included cardiac failure, coronary artery disease, myocardial fibrosis, breast neoplasm, hypercholesterolemia, cholecystitis chronic, pancreatitis chronic, and vascular encephalopathy. At baseline, she had a BMI of 34.2 kg/m2 and an eGFR of 60 mL/min/1.73 m2. Concomitant medications at baseline included: acetylsalicylic acid (100 mg daily) and rosuvastatin (20 mg daily).

(b) (6)	
A 77-year-old White male experienced a fatal stroke on Study Day 207. The subject began the study in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg arm and completed Part 1, switching to aprocitentan 25 mg for Part 2 on Study Day 33. On the morning of Day 207, the subject was found in bed with "abrupt clinical signs of deep coma with noisy pathological breathing." Emergency responders confirmed death of the subject. The family doctor, who witnessed the subject's death, said to the relatives that he diagnosed the cause of death as stroke. An autopsy was not performed. The last available BP value on Day 201 was 128/70 mmHg. Ongoing medical conditions at Screening included angina pectoris, chronic cardiac failure, hypertensive heart disease, mitral valve disease, myocardial ischemia, dyslipidemia, chronic kidney disease, cerebral arteriosclerosis, and encephalopathy. At baseline, he had a BMI of 35.4 kg/m2 and an eGFR of 44 mL/min/1.73 m2. Concomitant medications at baseline included rosuvastatin (10 mg daily).
L1791: (b) (6)
L1792: A 79-year-old White male experienced left middle cerebral artery stroke resulting in hospitalization on Study Day 32, and death on Study Day 37. The subject began the study in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm and completed Part 1, starting Part 2 on Study Day 29. On Study Day 31, the subject was reported as normal by his wife. The next day, she found him on the bathroom floor, and he was taken to the emergency department 12 hours later, presenting with right-sided weakness, the inability follow commands, and aphasia. Tissue plasminogen activator was not administered due to being outside the treatment time criteria. Hemoglobin and hematocrit were within normal ranges, eGFR was stable at 50 mL/min/1.73 m2. On the same day, the subject was transferred to another hospital for possible mechanical thrombectomy, which ultimately was not attempted due to the core infarct being too large, and expiration of the treatment time window. The subject was then discharged to palliative care on Study Day 35 and died on Study Day 37. The last available BP value on Day 26 was 124/72 mmHg. Ongoing medical conditions at Screening included chronic kidney disease, aortic arteriosclerosis, peripheral edema, sleep apnea syndrome, peripheral venous disease, gastroesophageal reflux disease, and hyperparathyroidism. At baseline, he had a BMI of 36.6 kg/m2 and an eGFR of 47 mL/min/1.73 m2. Concomitant medications at baseline included acetylsalicylic acid (81 mg daily), omeprazole (20 mg daily), and montelukast sodium (10 mg daily).

L1828: **Table 144. Narratives for Adjudicated MACE and MACE-Plus, Safety Population, PRECISION Study**
L1829: Subject ID	Clinical Information
Part 1
(b) (6)	
The 77-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg arm experienced nonfatal ischemic stroke on Study Day 27. The subject was hospitalized due to sudden dysarthria. A brain CT showed the presence of a hypodense area with blurry borders in the area of mesencephalon at the left side towards thalamus, which correspond to subacute ischemic changes. Otherwise, there was no intracranial bleeding or expansive process. An MRI was not performed. The study drug was permanently discontinued on Day 28 due to ischemic stroke. The event remained unresolved and was assessed as related to the study drug. Relevant medical conditions were reported as hypertension, previous transient ischemic attack, type 2 diabetes mellitus, dyslipidemia, and CKD. At baseline, he had a BMI of 29.7 kg/m2, eGFR of 38 mL/min/1.73 m2, and NT-proBNP of 246 pg/mL.

(b) (6)	
The 75-year-old White female in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg arm experienced an SAE of subacute right medial occipital lobe infarct on Study Day 17. The subject noticed symptoms of floaters in both eyes but did not notify the site. On Study Day 48, an MRI of the head without contrast indicated probable late subacute infarct in the right medial occipital lobe, noted to be at least several weeks in age, which corresponded with the onset of her visual changes. There was no evidence of hemorrhage or recent infarct. The study drug was interrupted from Day 38-41. The event remained unresolved and was considered not related to the study drug. The subject restarted treatment on Study Day 41 and completed Part 2 and Part 3 of the study. Relevant medical conditions were reported as hypertension (40 years) and peripheral edema. At baseline, she had a BMI of 31 kg/m2 and an eGFR of 62 mL/min/1.73 m2.

(b) (6)	
The 72-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 12.5 mg arm experienced a serious event of nonfatal myocardial infarction on Study Day 19-23. The event resolved with treatment. No action with study drug was taken. The event was assessed as not related. On Study Day 95, the subject experienced a serious event of nonfatal myocardial infarction related to coronary artery bypass graft (CABG) surgery, which was performed the day prior. The event resolved with coronarography and bypass. No action with study drug was taken. The event was assessed as not related. Relevant ongoing medical conditions at Screening included chronic cardiac failure, angina, coronary artery disease, myocardial ischemia, hypercholesterolemia, peripheral
L1830: ## NDA 217686
## TRYVIO (aprocitentan)
L1831: Subject ID	Clinical Information
arterial occlusive disease, diabetic nephropathy, and diabetes. At baseline, he had a BMI of 32.7 kg/m2 and an eGFR of 60 mL/min/1.73 m2.
(b) (6)	A 56-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm experienced a serious event of nonfatal stroke on Study Day 4-23. The event resolved with sequelae (speech impaired and memory reduced). The study drug was permanently discontinued on Study Day 15. The event was assessed as not related. Ongoing medical conditions at Screening included type 2 diabetes, dyslipidemia, and tobacco use. At baseline, he had a BMI of 35.9kg/m2 and an eGFR of 100 mL/min/1.73 m2.
(b) (6)	A 67-year-old White male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm experienced an SAE of pulmonary edema, cardiac failure, and increased blood pressure on Study Day 8-12. The subject was admitted to the hospital with a diagnosis of heart failure with preserved ejection fraction (HFpEF) with flash pulmonary edema after experiencing increasing dyspnea. Blood pressure upon arrival was 220/104 mmHg and chest X-ray showed early pulmonary edema. The subject's NT-proBNP value during hospitalization was not reported. The subject recovered after treatment and the event was considered not related to the study drug. There was no action take with study treatment. On Study Day 23, the subject presented to the emergency department with severe chest pain and peripheral edema that resolved after treatment with transdermal nitroglycerin and furosemide. There was no action taken with study treatment and the event was considered not related to the study drug. Ongoing medical conditions at Screening included type 2 diabetes, dyslipidemia, history of transient ischemic attack, and peripheral edema. At baseline, he had a BMI of 38.1kg/m2 and an eGFR of 62 mL/min/1.73 m2.
(b) (6)	An 83-year-old White female in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm had an SAE of cardiac failure on Study Day 22-30. The subject was admitted to the hospital with a diagnosis of worsening of chronic heart failure after experiencing edema and dyspnea since study Day 11. The study drug was interrupted from Day 22-30 and restarted on Day 31. The subject recovered after treatment and the event was considered not related to the study drug. Ongoing medical conditions at Screening included atrial fibrillation, chronic heart failure, chronic kidney disease, type 2 diabetes, dyslipidemia, transient ischemic attack, and peripheral arterial occlusive disease. At baseline, she had a BMI of 37.1kg/m2 and an eGFR of 38 mL/min/1.73 m2.

(b) (6)	The 66-year-old Black male in the aprocitentan 25 mg arm experienced a serious event of nonfatal myocardial infarction on Study Day 41. The subject was hospitalized with a diagnosis of myocardial infarction of mild intensity (non-ST-elevation) after reporting edema, dyspnea, and chest pain for the prior 4 days. The event resolved after treatment. The study drug was permanently discontinued on Study Day 41. The event was assessed as not related. He was transferred to the cardiology unit and underwent a coronarography on Day 186 that revealed trivascular coronaropathy with occlusion of medio-proximal anterior interventricular artery. The same day, he underwent a coronary angioplasty plus stent, on common trunk and distal segment. The procedure was uncomplicated and regular; the subject was discharged on study Day 190. No action was taken with the study drug and the event was assessed as not related to study treatment. Relevant ongoing medical conditions at Screening included hypercholesterolemia, chronic kidney disease, peripheral vascular disorder, and type 2 diabetes. At baseline, he had a BMI of 27.8 kg/m2 and an eGFR of 30 mL/min/1.73 m2.

A 66-year-old White female in the apocitentan 25 mg arm experienced an SAE of congestive heart failure on Study Day 39-47. The subject was admitted to the hospital on Study Day 39 after experiencing a two-day history of dyspnea. Chest CTA showed bilateral pleural effusion and pulmonary edema secondary to heart failure. There was no pulmonary embolism or signs of infiltrates. On Day 41, transthoracic echocardiogram showed an ejection fraction of 50-55% with grade 3/4 diastolic dysfunction and right ventricular systolic pressure of 51 mmHg. A nuclear stress test showed an intermediate risk stress test due to left ventricular dilatation and transient ischemic dilatation, most consistent with either a dilated cardiomyopathy or could be seen with multivessel coronary disease and balanced ischemia. The subject recovered after treatment and the event was assessed as not related to the study drug. The study drug was permanently discontinued on study Day 39. Ongoing medical conditions at Screening included left ventricular hypertrophy, type 2 diabetes, and dyslipidemia. At baseline, he had a BMI of 34.6 kg/m2 and an eGFR of 91 mL/min/1.73 m2.

A 2D echocardiogram revealed a serious event of grade 2 left ventricular diastolic dysfunction of moderate intensity with preserved ejection fraction. The subject recovered after treatment and the event was assessed as not related to the study drug. No action was taken with the study treatment. Ongoing medical conditions at Screening included chronic kidney disease, coronary artery disease, type 2 diabetes, and dyslipidemia. A 72-year-old Black male in the apocitentan 25 mg arm experienced an SAE of pulmonary edema, congestive heart failure, and hypertensive urgency on Study Day 134 to 137 that led to drug interruption for the hypertensive urgency. The subject was admitted to the hospital with a diagnosis of congestive heart failure, pulmonary edema in the setting of hypertensive urgency, acute kidney injury on chronic kidney disease, and atrial flutter. Blood pressure upon arrival was reported as 209/60 mmHg and chest X-ray showed enlarged cardiac silhouette and pulmonary vasculature congestion. The subject recovered after treatment with intravenous (IV) furosemide, nitrates, and hydralazine. The study treatment was interrupted on Day 137 due to the hypertensive urgency. The event was considered not related to study drug. Prior to the study (at screening), the subject's individual background antihypertensive therapy included SBAT and hydralazine (50 mg twice daily) which was discontinued at that time, per study protocol. The subject was restarted on study treatment on Study Day 147, completing the study Parts 2 and 3 in the apocitentan 25 mg arm as scheduled.

L1880: ## 23. Labeling: Key Changes and Considerations
L1881: This Prescribing Information (PI) review includes a high-level summary of the rationale for major changes to the finalized PI as compared to the Applicant's draft PI (Table 148). The PI was reviewed to ensure that PI meets regulatory/statutory requirements, is consistent (if appropriate) with labeling guidance, conveys clinically meaningful and scientifically accurate information needed for the safe and effective use of the drug, and provides clear and concise information for the healthcare practitioner.
L1882: **Table 148. Key Labeling Changes and Considerations**
L1883: Full PI Sections1	Rationale for Major Changes to Finalized PI2 Compared to Applicant's Draft PI
BOXED WARNING	The Boxed Warning for embryo-fetal toxicity was revised for consistency with class labeling for this risk.
1 INDICATIONS AND USAGE	The Applicant's proposed indication was not substantively revised.
2 DOSAGE AND ADMINISTRATION	Revisions made in Dosage and Administration to reflect the decision (b) (4)
Language regarding pregnancy testing prior to initiation, during therapy and after discontinuation was harmonized with the REMS.
4 CONTRAINDICATIONS	Contraindications revised to reflect class labeling for embryo-fetal risk.
5 WARNINGS AND PRECAUTIONS	The subsection for embryo-fetal risk was harmonized with class labeling; a subsection for the TRYVIO REMS was also added.
The subsection for hepatotoxicity was revised to include additional description of the clinical trial experience and to include additional text for patients to report symptoms of hepatotoxicity.
The subsections for fluid retention and hemoglobin decrease were revised to include additional descriptions of the clinical trial experience.
A subsection for decreased sperm counts was added, consistent with class labeling.
6 ADVERSE REACTIONS	Details regarding (b) (4) were relocated to Section 5.
7 DRUG INTERACTIONS	N/A
8 USE IN SPECIFIC POPULATIONS (e.g., Pregnancy, Lactation, Females and Males of Reproductive Potential, Pediatric Use, Geriatric Use, Renal Impairment, Hepatic Impairment)	Language pertinent to Pregnancy, Lactation and Reproductive Potential were harmonized with class labeling.
pertinent information was incorporated into (b) (4) Warnings and Precautions.
9 DRUG ABUSE AND DEPENDENCE	In the Hepatic Impairment subsection, the proposed recommendation for patients with moderate hepatic impairment was revised to 'not recommended.'
10 OVERDOSAGE	N/A
12 CLINICAL PHARMACOLOGY	The applicants proposed text for this subsection was not substantively revised.
In the Mechanism of Action section, text thought not relevant or critical to describe the action of this product was removed.
The Pharmacodynamics and Pharmacokinetic subsections were revised to be consistent with current Guidances.
13 NONCLINICAL TOXICOLOGY	Details regarding carcinogenicity studies were added.
The 'Impairment of Fertility' section was revised to include additional data from studies with aprocitentan.
14 CLINICAL STUDIES	(b) (4)

(b) (4) In addition, the Applicant's table of results for the primary endpoint was updated to appropriately take into account assumptions for missing data. (b) (4) were removed and instead, a qualitative description of Part 3 describing the durability of the treatment effect on blood pressure was included.

17 PATIENT COUNSELING INFORMATION	The Patient Counseling section was revised to harmonize the pregnancy related language with class labeling, and to include text regarding the REMS.

L1890: ## 24. Postmarketing Requirements and Commitments
L1891: The following postmarketing requirement will be required to assess a signal of a serious risk of embryo-fetal toxicity with the use of aprocitentan:
L1892: A worldwide descriptive study that collects prospective and retrospective data in women exposed to aprocitentan during pregnancy to assess risk of pregnancy and maternal complications, adverse effects on the developing fetus and neonate, and adverse effects on the infant. Infant outcomes will be assessed through at least the first year of life. The minimum number of patients will be specified in the protocol.

L1954: Scatter plot showing the relationship between AUC_{ss} (area under the concentration-time curve at steady-state, \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL}) on the x-axis and Change from SiSBP baseline (mmHg) on the y-axis. Data points are color-coded by dose: 12.5 mg (red dots) and 25 mg (teal dots). A linear regression line is shown, along with a shaded confidence interval. The regression equation is y = -13.7 - 0.0296x, with R^2_{adj} = 0.00198, R^2 = 0.00435, and P = 0.176.
L1955: Figure 2 illustrates the PRECISION Study Design, a timeline showing the study phases and visits (V1 to V16).
Phase 1: Screening and Run-in
V1: Screening (Up to 8 weeks at the latest).
Week -8 at the latest: Individual background antihypertensive medication.
Week -4 to Week -2: Standard background antihypertensive therapy (\ge 4 weeks before Run-in).
V2: Screening (4 weeks).
V3: Run-in (4 weeks). Add placebo.
Phase 2: Randomisation (V4)
Subjects are randomized into three arms:
Aprocitentan 25 mg
Placebo
Aprocitentan 12.5 mg
Phase 3: Double-Blind Treatment (Part 1, V5 to V6)
Double-Blind, 4 weeks. Subjects continue standard background therapy.
Phase 4: Single-Blind Treatment (Part 2, V7 to V10)
Single-Blind, 32 weeks. All subjects receive Aprocitentan 25 mg. Subjects continue standard background therapy.
Phase 5: Double-Blind Withdrawal (Part 3, V11 to V13)
Double-Blind Withdrawal, 12 weeks. Subjects are rerandomized (V11, Week 38).
Subjects randomized to Aprocitentan 25 mg receive Aprocitentan 25 mg.
Subjects randomized to Placebo receive Placebo.
Phase 6: Safety Follow-up (FU, V14 to V16)
Safety Follow-up, 30 days. Subjects continue standard background therapy.
L1956: Diagram illustrating the Testing Strategy. The overall significance level is \alpha=0.025. The first null hypothesis, H_{10}, is tested with \mu_1 = \mu_0. The second null hypothesis, H_{20}, is tested with \mu_2 = \mu_0. Both H_{10} and H_{20} are tested at level 1. The third null hypothesis, H_{30}, is tested with V_1 = V_0, and is only tested if H_{10} or H_{20} is rejected.

L1967: Figure 14 illustrates the metabolic pathways of Aprocitentan (ACT-132577). Aprocitentan undergoes extensive metabolism, leading to 15 identified metabolites (M1 through M26).
The pathways include:
Pathway 1 (M10, M13, M26): Aprocitentan is converted to M10 (ACT-080803). M10 is further metabolized to M13 (via a glucuronidation reaction, indicated by OGLUC) and M26 (via a hydroxylation reaction, indicated by OH).
Pathway 2 (M2, M4, M11): Aprocitentan is converted to M2 (via a hydroxylation reaction, indicated by OH). M2 is further metabolized to M4 (via a glucuronidation reaction, indicated by OGLUC) and M11 (via a hydroxylation reaction, indicated by OH).
Pathway 3 (M5, M6, M16): Aprocitentan is converted to M5 (via a hydroxylation reaction, indicated by OH). M5 is further metabolized to M6 (via a hydroxylation reaction, indicated by OH) and M16 (via a glucuronidation reaction, indicated by OGLUC).
Pathway 4 (M21, M15): Aprocitentan is converted to M21 (ACT-1005-9435) (via a hydroxylation reaction, indicated by OH). M21 is further metabolized to M15 (via a glucuronidation reaction, indicated by OGLUC).
Pathway 5 (M8, M9, M22): Aprocitentan is converted to M8 (via a hydroxylation reaction, indicated by OH). M8 is further metabolized to M9 (via a hydroxylation reaction, indicated by OH) and M22 (via a glucuronidation reaction, indicated by OGLUC).
The structures of Aprocitentan and all 15 metabolites (M1 through M26) are shown in the figure.
L1968: Figure 15. Chemical Structures of (b) (4) and (b) (4)
Source: Applicant's submission—aprocitentan (ACT-132577) Drug Substance Impurities
L1969: Figure 16 illustrates the proposed metabolic pathways of Aprocitentan (ACT-132577). The central molecule is Aprocitentan, which undergoes various metabolic transformations leading to metabolites M1 through M22.
The pathways include:
Hydrolysis: Aprocitentan is hydrolyzed to M1 (ACT-080803) and M21 (ACT-1005-9435).
Glucuronidation: Aprocitentan is glucuronidated to M3, M9, M10, M11, M13, M15, M16, M18, M20, and M22.
Other transformations: M1 is further metabolized to M2, M4, M5, M6, and M11. M21 is metabolized to M15.
The figure shows the chemical structures of Aprocitentan and all identified metabolites (M1-M22).

The Y-axis ranges from 0 to 800 \mumol/min, and the X-axis ranges from 0 to 10 hours. Error bars represent Mean \pm SD.
The top row shows results for the 10 mg dose group. The middle row shows results for the 25 mg dose group. The bottom row shows results for the 50 mg dose group.
In all graphs, the ACT-123577 groups generally show slightly higher mean sodium excretion rates compared to their respective placebo groups, particularly around 2 hours post-dose. However, the differences are small and within the range of variability (SD).

L1975: Scatter plot showing the relationship between AUC_{ss} (area under the concentration-time curve at steady-state, X-axis, in \mu\text{g}\cdot\text{h}/\text{mL}) and Change from SiSBP baseline (sitting systolic blood pressure, Y-axis, in mmHg). A shaded gray area indicates the confidence interval for the regression line.
